## 8.6 Business Models for IoT

Successful companies are increasingly paying attention to business modeling as a management tool that provides an overview of the company’s components and helps managers develop the business ([Dijkman et al., 2015](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref24)). Some researchers link business model applications to enhancing competitive advantage ([Johnson et al., 2008](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref39)). The importance of a business model has increased over time with the development of technology and the spread of e-commerce and the development of its concept until it is now seen as the way in which the company performs its tasks to generate revenue ([Wirtz et al., 2016](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref65)). In addition, it may be seen as an alternative or complementary tool to traditional analytical methods.

### 8.6.1 Business Model Framework for IoT Applications

Business models of traditional businesses and digital businesses provide a strong foundation for understanding the business models of emerging IoT applications.  155. According to [Li and Xu (2013)](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref44), an IoT business model is a multi-dimensional structure that considers technology, industry, policy, and strategy dimensions. The emergence of new business applications through new technologies and digital transformation requires the creation of new business models that help deliver, disseminate, and monetize products/services ([Amit and Zott, 2000](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref7)).

Table 8.4 Components of IoT Business Models [Return to text.⏎](#ch08.xhtml#R_24032-0872_ch008_tab_004)

 
| **Components** || **Bhushan and Agrawal (2020)** || **Ashton (2009)** || **Chen (2012** || **Delmastro (2012)** || **Hui et al. (2017)** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Key partners** || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Key activities** || ✓ || ✓ ||  || ✓ || ✓ |
| **Key resources** || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ ||  ||  |
| **Value propositions** || ✓ || ✓ ||  ||  || ✓ |
| **Channels** || ✓ || ✓ ||  ||  || ✓ |
| **Customer segments** || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ |
| **Cost structure** || ✓ ||  ||  ||  || ✓ |
| **Revenue streams** || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ || ✓ |

To develop a framework for IoT business models, some researchers have used the framework proposed by [Osterwalder and Pigneur (2010)](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref51) as a starting point to uncover the main components of a business model (Uckelmann et al., 2010; [Dijkman et al., 2015](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref24)) (see [Table 8.4](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch008_tab_004)).

[Table 8.4](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch008_tab_004) shows the different components of business models that have been developed from value offer to value capture [(Dijkman et al., 2015](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref24); [Li and Xu, 2013](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref44); Uckelmann et al., 2010).

*Value proposition* refers to the service provided and the aspects that add value to it, such as brand, price, and speed of delivery, for example. Moreover, the data collected and processed from physical objects are part of providing services to the user. Channels and customer relationships can also be considered as one of the most important factors in providing the service. Value proposition is also related to delivery service and evaluation of customer satisfaction after completing the exchange process.

*The customer perspective* refers to customers that the company addresses, and the customer relationships are defined through different communication channels, direct or indirect.

*The financial perspective* refers to the revenue structure, costs, and methods of generating revenue such as asset sales, subscription, usage fees, and advertising among many other elements. The business model includes elements related to cost and choosing the appropriate strategy such as cost-effectiveness strategy or differentiation strategy. Finally, IoT business models reveal the type of partners, supplier networks, and alliances that companies create with external sources.

 156. In IoT, *key resources* including human, intellectual, and physical resources are considered as the core sources of a company’s business. Business models describe the activities a company follows to create, market, and capture value.

## 8.7 Applied Study of IoT/Smart Cities

One of the important trends in the application of IoT is its ability to manage public facilities in an automated way. The study of [Talari et al. (2017)](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref62) revealed that based on smart technologies, especially in the field of infrastructure and IoT, ([Alavi et al., 2018](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref4)) cities can be described as being able to respond and provide services through their various facilities in a more intelligent way than is usual or provided by traditional cities ([Joshi and Gupta, 2019](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref40)).

Smart cities use various forms of applications in infrastructure, monitoring equipment, sensor systems, smart energy use, pollution prevention, healthcare, and smart security, among many others ([Hui et al., 2017](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref34); [Jain and Jain, 2022](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref38); [Shafie-Khah et al., 2016](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref57)). Smart cities use IoT, especially sensors, to collect data on the behavior of drivers in case they violate traffic rules by placing monitoring devices in crowded areas to monitor traffic in addition to installing devices to detect the level of air pollution and reveal the identities of people, which improves the cleanliness of cities and adds a civilized character to human behavior ([Botta et al., 2016](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref16)). IoT supports the smart city project through many applications in the field of controlling energy consumption and kitchen appliances such as refrigerators and washing machines. These applications can be used in caring for elderly patients and monitoring health developments ([Jain and Jain, 2022](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref38)). At the social level, various social networks help in real-time and continuous communication through text messages, voice, or video ([Samih, 2019](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref56); [Gubbi et al., 2013](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref28)).

Smart cities use sensor systems to detect water pollution, wind speed, weather conditions, temperature, and flood risk to provide data for taking preventive measures and warning citizens of their consequences on their health and daily lives. Smart cities can use sensors to detect malfunctions in the drinking water distribution system and water leaks in tanks, pipelines, or buildings ([Botta et al., 2016](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref16)).

The Smart Community Forum announced the list of the best smart cities in the world 2023, from the USA, Canada, Australia, Brazil, and Vietnam ([www.icfsummit.com](http://www.icfsummit.com)). The selected cities are models of economic, social and even cultural development in the age of digital transformation. Binh Duong Smart City in Vietnam was selected as the best city for the third time in a row from the list of the best smart cities in the world. This city is open to the world and has an ecosystem and is a center of science and technology. One of the most important projects is to make the city green. The government agency oversees the development of this city with the participation of the academic sector and entrepreneurs, where one million people in Vietnam are expected to live. The city has developed a communications network and provided free Wi-Fi for all users. The city focused on connecting government departments such as government buildings, universities, public and private schools, transportation and shops with wireless networks for employees, students, and visitors. The city focused on sustainable economic development through cooperation between the government, universities, and industrialists. The city provides technology laboratories  157. for education and industry, including laboratories for lighting, electricity, mechanics, communications, robotics, AI systems, 3D printing, laser, scanning, and digital control. The city hosts the first business incubator, providing all the necessary facilities, support, training, and communication for entrepreneurs and start-ups ([The Intelligent Community Forum, 2024](#ch08.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp008_ref36)).

## 8.8 Conclusion

The Fourth Industrial Revolution has contributed to the development of the industrial and business sector, as statistics indicate that the number of things that are connected to each other will increase threefold in the coming years, and IoT is expected to become an important source of profits and revenues in the foreseeable future. Industry4.0 has contributed to the development of the industrial and business sector.

Although IoT has several benefits, it faces major challenges on technical, economic, social, legal, and security levels. Technically, devices may differ and vary among themselves depending on innovative companies, the standards they follow, and the protocols they propose for communication. Heterogeneity is observed in operating systems, privacy, processing large amounts of data, reliability, security, and many other areas. Since commercial applications directly serve consumers and business customers, they require a high level of trust in the operating systems they rely on. In the absence of this trust, the end consumer or user will be frustrated, change their behavior, and move away from using these platforms. Therefore, it is recommended to gain customer trust by providing reliable services while maintaining information security and privacy.

This chapter can be a starting point for future research on qualitative and quantitative studies to explore the impact of value, offering, and collection as components of business models on companies. The study can be useful for managers and decision makers in creating new business models for digital companies aiming to implement IoT applications.

# 9 Robotic Process AutomationStreamlining Business Processes for Operational Excellence

Magesh Sankar, Vijayalakshmi Sivaramakrishnan, Palamadai Subramanian Rajakumar, S. Geetha, and R. Manikandan

DOI: [10.1201/9781003561873-9](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003561873-9)

## 9.1 Introduction

In today’s fast-paced, competitive business environment, organizations face tremendous pressure to enhance efficiency, reduce operational costs, and deliver high-quality services. Robotic process automation (RPA) is a transformative technology that automatically performs repetitive, rule-based tasks, allowing businesses to optimize their operations and focus on their strategic goals. With this concept, RPA bots mimic human-like interactions with digital systems for extraction, processing, and generation of reports at high speed and accuracy. This also eliminates human error while significantly reducing processing time and operation costs, thereby making RPA a critical component in any digital transformation strategy \[[1](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref1), [2](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref2)\]. The first to adopt RPA have been the banking, healthcare, insurance, manufacturing, and retail industries, leveraging the platform to automate their back-office processes and streamline the workflow. For instance, in banking, loan-processing times have plummeted; in healthcare, it is used to manage patient records and billing systems, allowing for seamless integration with the existing enterprise systems. The RPA-based non-intrusive modernization method further accelerates its adoption in a variety of domains \[[3](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref3), [4](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref4)\].

RPA is very much related to the advancements made in AI and ML. Intelligent automation is the integration of AI and RPA, which allows systems to analyze unstructured data, understand context, and even make decisions. The same ability has brought about a new wave of possibilities in complicated workflow automation, such as fraud detection in finance and predictive maintenance in manufacturing. In fact, resource optimization and enhanced agility in operations have become the backbone of business process optimization \[[5](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref5)\].

Despite the numerous benefits of RPA, challenges exist for its adoption. The initial investment cost is too high, people resist change, and standardization does not  162. exist. Moreover, while RPA is outstanding in rule-based work, much work is to be done in AI integration in dealing with highly dynamic and judgment-based activities. The cloud-based RPA platforms and explainable AI models have addressed the above-mentioned challenges. Scalability, transparency, and cost-effectiveness make them one of the best solutions \[[6](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref6)\]. In customer service, RPA has been very helpful as well. Automated chatbots, email response systems, and personalized communication tools help businesses provide 24/7 customer support, resolve queries promptly, and enhance user experiences. These technologies not only enhance customer satisfaction but also increase retention rates, thus reflecting the direct impact of RPA on business outcomes \[[7](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref7)\]. In addition to operational efficiency, RPA contributes to sustainability by reducing resource consumption and, therefore, minimizing environmental impact. Reduced paper usage, energy consumption, and overall waste make automation a valuable tool for organizations committed to sustainability goals.

Furthermore, the automation of routine work will enable employees to engage in creative and strategic work that will promote creativity and job satisfaction \[[8](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref8)\]. Another major driver in RPA adoption is regulatory compliance. Automated systems can scan for compliance requirements, watch audit trails, and check if the standard conditions are followed to reduce the risk of non-compliance, particularly in finance, healthcare, and other heavily regulated sectors \[[9](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref9)\]. All this happens within businesses, yet the future technology that will transform the operational paradigms in the digital age remains RPA. Because it brings both productivity and cost savings together with better experiences for customers, RPA remains a vital asset for achieving operational excellence \[[10](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref10)\].

### 9.2 Research Aim and Scope

This research aims to develop an advanced RPA operative management solution to enable efficient job scheduling, resource allocation, and job execution monitoring, thus improving business operations. The current research aims to provide novel approaches for robotic process automation systems that will assist in addressing the problem of increasing demand for automation in retail, low scalability, and expansion of RPA systems so that firms can achieve operational excellence and transform digitally.

#### 9.2.1 Research Motivations and Objectives

*   **Growing Demand for Automation:** The need for intelligent RPA solutions is driven by the growing requirement for process automation to save costs and improve efficiency.
*   **Operational Challenges**: The dynamic resource allocation and real-time flexibility of current RPA systems are limited, requiring sophisticated optimization approaches.
*   **Scalability Problems:** Companies need scalable frameworks that can effectively manage big datasets and intricate procedures.
*   **Enhancement of Performance:** For sustainable expansion, performance measures, including job completion time, resource use, and energy efficiency, must be improved.
*    163. **Technological Advancements:** Integration of ML and reinforcement learning offers a chance to develop more intelligent RPA systems that optimize themselves.

The key objectives to overcome the above problems are as follows:

*   Design an adaptive resource management system that dynamically balances workloads and resources using reinforcement learning techniques like SARSA-TSA.
*   ML methods such as APSO and XGBoost are used for efficient job planning and prioritization to reduce latency and increase throughput.
*   Develop a scalable framework that can manage complex processes and a variety of workloads from different sectors.
*   Enhance the performance of the overall system by optimizing low latency, improving job completion time, and achieving maximum energy conservation.
*   Develop performance monitoring tools and dashboards, along with automated ones that keep track of system performance and identify anomalies, in turn helping to take anticipatory measures using the ARIMA-WOA algorithm.
*   Compare existing methodologies such as GRA and GA on parameters such as throughput, resource utilization, and energy usage to prove that the presented technique is superior.

#### 9.2.2 Research contributions

*   An intelligent operational management framework that includes integration of ML and reinforcement learning for RPA systems with efficient allocation of resources and task scheduling.
*   SARSA-TSA for the adaptive resource optimization model: dynamic allocation of resources with scalability and flexibility improvements.
*   Feature engineering, opti-filter XGBoost, and APSO to improve predictive accuracy and system performance.
*   The novel ARIMA-WOA-based monitoring shows considerable improvements in performance metrics such as task completion time, latency, throughput, resource utilization, and energy efficiency.
*   A comparative analysis with existing approaches like GRA and GA will be provided to show the superiority of the proposed framework in handling complex and dynamic workflows.
*   Real-time monitoring and control system with proactive anomaly detection and performance optimization.

## 9.3 literature survey

RPA emerged as a disruptive technology in the pursuit of streamlining business processes and achieving operational excellence. Proper exploration of how it has been developed, adopted, and integrated into so many industries is required to understand  164. its capability. This chapter provides an in-depth review of some of the relevant research studies in the field of study. The author \[[11](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref11)\] published a systematic mapping study investigating the relationship that exists between scientific research and industrial applications of RPA. They expose the aspect of how RPA reduces human interaction in repetitive tasks, leading to efficiency and accuracy benefits. They conclude by highlighting the integration of RPA, AI, and ML technologies where complexities in operations would help answer the challenges. Among the identified application areas were finance and healthcare, which benefited immensely from the introduction of RPA. The authors \[[12](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref12)\] provide a systematic literature review of RPA adoption, shedding light on both its opportunities and barriers. Their work categorizes RPA applications across various industries and emphasizes its transformative role in optimizing operational processes. The study addresses critical challenges such as resistance to change, high implementation costs, and technical limitations, which organizations must overcome to realize the full potential of RPA. The authors \[[13](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref13)\] provide a framework for an analysis of RPA technologies through a systematic review of the available literature. This research focuses on the methodologies for implementing RPA effectively in business processes and its application in terms of gaining operational efficiency. It further sheds light on some newer trends, such as the integration of RPA with AI, which further enhances its capabilities for handling unstructured data processing and decision-making activities. An author \[[14](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref14)\] investigates the role of RPA as a driver of digital transformation. The research demonstrates its potential to automate back-office processes, reduce costs, and increase productivity. The author stresses that RPA allows organizations to transform workflows without changing the existing systems, which makes it a good choice for companies that want to have cost-effective digital transformation. The author \[[15](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref15)\] concentrates on the banking sector, where he demonstrates how RPA reduces the complexities of operations in loan processing and customer service. This research study provides cases that depict the effects of RPA on processing times and service quality that will enhance satisfaction for customers and efficiency of operations.

The author \[[16](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref16)\] discusses how RPA can be combined with platforms like Power Apps to rethink operational efficiency and data quality. The study also states how RPA can accomplish routine and rule-based operations efficiently, thereby enabling the organization to achieve higher data accuracy and avoid errors to a great extent. Through this integration, the workers will be free to execute the strategic and creative work that contributes to the entire growth of the organization. The author \[[17](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref17)\] explores the cloud-based RPA capabilities, of which he mainly focuses on Appian’s BPM platform. The chapter addresses the scalability and cost-effectiveness of cloud RPA solutions for companies to deploy their automation with little investment in infrastructure quickly. This study showed how cloud RPA makes it easy to integrate with existing systems and hence offers a viable option for those organizations looking to scale their operations. The authors \[[18](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref18)\] describe RPA’s strategy in automation across industries. The research shows its effectiveness in enhancing efficiency in operations through the automation of rule-based processes, hence reducing errors and increasing productivity. The author discusses how RPA impacts transformation in the retail and manufacturing industries where there are repetitive tasks. The authors \[[19](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref19)\] discuss innovations in intelligent automation. The research focuses on  165. integrating RPA with AI. Their work exhibits more sophisticated use cases, including data unstructured processing and enhancement of decision-making capabilities. The authors \[[20](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref20)\] suggest that AI-based RPA gives companies more accuracy and pace in terms of answering the more complex business needs. The author evaluates how RPA, along with AI and ML, facilitates the change in the mode of doing business. A few of the more complex use cases presented in this chapter are predictive analytics, anomaly detection, and customer-specific interactions. He further underscores the role of RPA in innovation, better resource allocation, and gaining operational agility.

This literature review demonstrates the evolution of RPA from being used as a mere tool to automate essential business functions toward becoming an integral component of every digital transformation strategy. Upon integration with AI and ML, RPA resolves those complex operational challenges in dealing with scalability, accuracy, and efficiency. High upfront costs and change adversity remain as barriers, but with regard to cloud-based RPA and intelligent automation, reasonable solutions are presented. These studies highlight the potential of RPA to improve efficiency of business processes and ensure operational excellence.

## 9.4 proposed methodology

To overcome the obstacles in optimizing business processes for operational efficiency using RPA, one is required to adopt a thorough and systematic solution. The approach suggested incorporates around five significant stages. These include data collection, preprocessing, feature engineering, scheduling and resource allocation, and operational management. All these stages together constitute a basic set of components for the development, deployment, and modification of RPA systems for various types of organizations. The general framework of this research is presented in [Figure 9.1](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-001).

## 9.5 DATA COLLECTION

The retail dataset obtained from Kaggle contains a lot of ready-to-use transaction data that is needed to understand and analyze applicable retail business processes. Apart from satisfying the business requirement, this dataset also includes details of products, motivation of customers, sale deals, and the performance of shops, which makes it very suitable for automating business processes. In this research, the scope of data collection concentrates on fetching information regarding product sales, customers, and operating parameters. These data points are critical in expressing the desirability of RPA systems in solving retail problems. This organized research and analysis aimed to provide a strong basis for enhancing data processes in the area of retail business ([https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/manjeetsingh/retaildataset](https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/manjeetsingh/retaildataset)).

## 9.6 PREPROCESSING

Preprocessing ensures that the dataset is clean, consistent, and prepared for further analysis. RPA is used to streamline these processes. The following steps are implemented.

![Figure 9.1Overall architecture of this research. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure9_1.jpg)

Figure 9.1Overall architecture of this research. [Return to text.⏎](#ch09.xhtml#R_24032-0872-009-Figure-001)

 166.  

### 9.6.1 Data Cleaning

*   Handling Missing Values: For numerical attributes, missing values are imputed using the mean or median:
    
    $x^{\\prime} = \\frac{\\sum\_{i - 1}^{n}x\_{i}}{n}$ (9.1)
    

where *x*^′ is the imputed value, *x*[*i*] are non-missing values, and *n* is the total count of non-missing values.

*   For categorical attributes, the mode is used for imputation.
*   Removing Duplicates: Rows are checked for duplicate entries based on unique identifiers such as transaction ID.

 167.  

### 9.6.2 Data Transformation

*   Normalization: Numerical attributes are normalized to bring them to a common scale using min-max scaling:

$x\_{\\text{scaled}} = \\frac{x - x\_{\\text{min}}}{x\_{\\text{max}} - x\_{\\text{min}}}$ (9.2)

where *x*[scaled] is the normalized value, *x* is the original value, *x*[min] is the minimum value, and *x*[max] is the maximum value in the dataset.

*   Aggregating transactional data to calculate customer-centric metrics:

$\\text{~Total~Spend~(per~Customer)~} - \\sum\_{i = 1}^{N}\\text{~Transaction~Value~N}\_{i}$ (9.3)

where *N* is the number of transactions per customer.

### 9.6.3 Data Integration

*   If additional datasets are used, relational joins are performed using primary keys (e.g., customer ID).

### 9.6.4 Automation Using RPA

*   Preprocessing workflows are automated with RPA tools, such as UiPath or Automation Anywhere, to ensure efficient and error-free operations.

This preprocessing approach will ensure the readiness of the dataset for appropriate feature engineering and analysis, robust insights, and operational improvement.

## 9.7 Feature Engineering

Feature engineering means coming up with novel features that better represent what is being automated, such as transforming categorical data into numerical features or aggregating time-based data for efficiency.

To address feature recovery issues, model interpretability and accuracy must be improved. We propose a novel technique called “Opti-Filter XGBoost,” which integrates the “Filter-based APSO-XGBoost” method for feature selection and engineering. This method improves the predictive exactness and interpretability of the perfect by identifying essential characteristics for grouping consumers in credit datasets. Using the value of rough statistics according to the class, this filter-based feature selection approach assesses the value of an attribute. By evaluating their independence and ensuring that there are no statistical connections, it assesses the capacity to forecast an attribute’s value based on the class value. Statistical measurements for each class are used to evaluate a feature’s value. Assume that 𝒳 = {𝒳[1],𝒳[2],…,𝒳[*n*]}, then the feature set with *n* features is represented by 𝒳 = {𝒳[1],𝒳[2],…,𝒳[*n*]}, while *k*  168. classes are represented by ℂ = {ℂ[1],ℂ[2],…ℂ[*k*]}. A feature 𝒳[*i*] has a significance score 𝒮𝒳[*i*] that is determined as follows:

$\\mathcal{SX}\_{i} = \\sum\_{j = 1}^{k}{\\mathbb{P}}\\left( {\\mathbb{C}}\_{j} \\right) \\cdot stat\\left( {\\mathcal{X}\_{i},{\\mathbb{C}}\_{j}} \\right)$ (9.4)

Here,

*   ℙ(ℂ[*j*]) denotes the probability of class ℂ[*j*] and
*   *s**t**a**t*(𝒳[*i*],ℂ[*j*]) is a statistical function that measures how relevant 𝒳[*i*] is for ℂ[*j*], such as mutual information, chi-square, or correlation coefficient. Mutual data (*M**D*) is calculated as follows:

$MD\\left( {\\mathcal{X},{\\mathbb{C}}} \\right) = \\sum\_{\\mathcal{X},{\\mathbb{C}}}{\\mathbb{P}}\\left( {\\mathcal{X},{\\mathbb{C}}} \\right)\\log\\left( \\frac{{\\mathbb{P}}\\left( {\\mathcal{X},{\\mathbb{C}}} \\right)}{{\\mathbb{P}}\\left( \\mathcal{X} \\right){\\mathbb{P}}\\left( {\\mathbb{C}} \\right)} \\right)$ (9.5)

### 9.7.1 Adaptive Particle Swarm Optimization

The APSO technique is used to improve model performance by changing the velocity and location of the particles. The particle velocity *v*[*i*] at time (*T*+1) is given below:

*v*[*i*](*T*+1) = *W**v*[*i*](*T*) + ℨ[1]*r*[1](ℙ[*i*]−𝒳[*i*](*T*)) + ℨ[2]*r*[2](ℙ[*i*]−𝒳[*i*](*T*)) (9.6)

where *r*[1] and *r*[2] are random values between 0 and 1, *W* is the inertia weight, and ℨ[1] and ℨ[2] are the acceleration coefficients for the cognitive components. The particle position is given as follows:

𝒳[*i*](*T*+1) = 𝒳[*i*](*T*) + *v*[*i*](*T*+1). (9.7)

To improve convergence, APSO also uses local density and distance data. The local density of a particle can be determined using the equation below:

$\\gamma\_{i} = \\sum\_{j = 1,j \\neq i}^{n}\\exp\\left( {- \\frac{\\parallel \\mathcal{X}\_{i} - \\mathcal{X}\_{j} \\parallel^{2}}{d\_{\\mathbb{C}}^{2}}} \\right)$ (9.8)

where *Δ*[*i*] is the proximity to a particle with a higher density and is defined as follows:

$\\Delta\_{i} = \\min\\limits\_{j:\\gamma\_{j} > \\gamma\_{i}} \\parallel \\mathcal{X}\_{i} - \\mathcal{X}\_{j} \\parallel$ (9.9)

The goal function on which the XGBoost approach is built lowers the prediction error while preserving model complexity. The expression used for the function’s purposes is as follows:

$L = \\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}\\ \\left( {y\_{i},y\_{i}} \\right) + \\sum\_{k = 1}^{k}\\Omega\\left( \\mathcal{F}\_{k} \\right),$ (9.10)

 169. The loss function 𝓁(*y*[*i*],*ŷ*[*l*]) measures the difference between the actual target *y*[*i*] and the predicted value $\\hat{y\_{i}}$, whereas *Ω*(ℱ[*k*]) is a regularization factor that penalizes overly complex models. The regularization term is given by the following:

$\\Omega\\left( \\mathcal{F}\_{k} \\right) = \\eta N + \\frac{1}{2}\\Lambda\\left. ||W \\right.||^{2}$ (9.11)

In the decision tree, *N* represents the leaf count, *W* represents the leaf weight vector, *η*  represents the penalty for adding extra leaves, and *Λ* represents the penalty for excessive weight values. These components work together to ensure that model complexity and accuracy are balanced. The contributions from each *k* tree are added together to provide predictions in XGBoost:

$\\hat{y\_{i}} = \\sum\_{k = 1}^{k}\\mathcal{F}\_{k}\\left( \\mathcal{X}\_{i} \\right)$ (9.12)

where 𝒳[*i*] represents the input feature vector for sample *i*. To simplify optimization, the loss function is approximated using the Taylor expansion up to the second order:

$L^{(T)} \\approx \\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}\\left\\lbrack {\\mathfrak{h}\_{i}\\mathcal{F}\_{k}\\left\\lbrack \\mathcal{X}\_{i} \\right\\rbrack + \\frac{1}{2}\\mathfrak{g}\_{i}\\mathcal{F}\_{k}^{2}\\left\\lbrack \\mathcal{X}\_{i} \\right\\rbrack} \\right\\rbrack + \\Omega\\left( \\mathcal{F}\_{k} \\right),$ (9.13)

The following are the first- and second-order derivatives of the loss function compared to the predictions, represented by 𝔥[*i*] and 𝔤[*i*], respectively:

$\\mathfrak{h}\_{i} = \\frac{\\partial\\mathcal{l}\\left( {y\_{i},y\_{i}} \\right)}{\\partial y\_{i}},\\text{\\quad\\;}\\mathfrak{g}\_{i} = \\frac{\\partial^{2}\\mathcal{l}\\left( {y\_{i},y\_{i}} \\right)}{\\partial{y\_{i}}^{2}}$ (9.14)

The node is divided using a decision tree, and the benefit from the split node is computed as follows:

$gain = \\frac{\\mathfrak{G}\_{l}^{2}}{H\_{l} + \\Lambda} + \\frac{\\mathfrak{G}\_{r}^{2}}{H\_{r} + \\Lambda} - \\frac{\\left( {\\mathfrak{G}\_{l} + \\mathfrak{G}\_{r}} \\right)^{2}}{H\_{l} + H\_{r} + \\Lambda}$, (9.15)

where 𝔊[*l*]. and 𝔊[*r*] represent the gradient sums for the left and right child nodes, respectively, and *H*[*l*] and *H*[*r*] indicate the corresponding Hessian sums. The optimal weight of a leaf node is determined as follows:

$W\_{j} = - \\frac{\\mathfrak{G}\_{j}}{H\_{i} + \\Lambda}$ (9.16)

 170. To assess the significance of feature 𝒳[*j*], the cumulative gain of all splits including it is calculated:

*i**m**p**o**r**t**a**n**c**e*(𝒳[*j*]) = ∑[*s**p**l**i**t**s* *i**n**v**o**l**v**i**n**g* 𝒳[*j*]]*g**a**i**n* (9.17)

To optimize the performance of RPA, tasks and resources need to be appropriately scheduled. This includes mapping tasks to the available robots and optimum usage of resources. After the deployment of RPA processes, it is necessary to monitor and manage ongoing processes so that they run smoothly. It will include monitoring task progress, resource utilization, and handling failures or exceptions in real time.

## 9.8 Resource Allocation and Scheduling

In the context of RPA, this is integrated into the process of allocating computational, network, and storage resources efficiently in real time among different tasks. SARSA is one of the popular RL algorithms that provide effective dynamic allocation of resources that learn and adapt to alterations in task demands and availability. This section explains this application in detail toward resource allocation through SARSA in RPA systems to ensure operational excellence.

SARSA is an on-policy reinforcement learning algorithm in which the agent learns from the action that it has under its current policy, continuously updating its state-action value function *Q*(*S, A*). The key aim of using SARSA in resource allocation is the determination of the optimal policy that can minimize system latency, maximize throughput, and balance resource utilization with respect to how tasks are processed efficiently in real time. In the RPA system, resource allocation refers to a process where available resources (be it computational power, network bandwidth, or memory) are assigned a task, all within constraints about task priority, resource availability, and real-time performance. The SARSA algorithm updates the action value function *Q*(*S*,*A*) as follows:

*Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*])←*Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*])+*α*(*R*[*t*]+*γ**Q*(*S*[*t* + 1],*A*[*t* + 1])−*Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*])) (9.18)

where *S*[*t*] is the state at time  *t*, representing the current configuration of the system, *A*[*t*] is the action taken at time *t*, representing the resource allocation decision, *R*[*t*] is the immediate reward received after executing action *A*[*t*] in state *S*[*t*]. *γ* is the discount factor, which gives weight to future rewards, and *α* is the learning rate, determining how much the agent updates its knowledge from new experiences. *Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*]) is the action value function that represents the expected cumulative reward of taking action *A*[*t*] in state *S*[*t*]. In SARSA, the state *S*[*t*] represents the current condition of the system, including available resources and the status of tasks. A state can be represented by a vector of key performance indicators (KPIs) and system status variables that are relevant to resource allocation:

*S*[*t*] = \[*T**a**s**k* *Q**u**e**u**e* *L**e**n**g**t**h*, *A**v**a**i**l**a**b**l**e* *R**e**s**o**u**r**c**e**s*, *T**a**s**k* *P**r**i**o**r**i**t**i**e**s*,

*C**P**U* *u**s**a**g**e*, *M**e**m**o**r**y* *u**s**a**g**e*, *N**e**t**w**o**r**k* *l**a**t**e**n**c**y*, *T**a**s**k* *C**o**m**p**l**e**t**i**o**n* *T**i**m**e*\] (9.19)

 171. where *T**a**s**k* *Q**u**e**u**e* *L**e**n**g**t**h* represents the number of tasks waiting for resource allocation, *Available Resources* indicates the amount of computational power, memory, and network bandwidth that is available at the time, *T**a**s**k* *P**r**i**o**r**i**t**i**e**s* show the priority levels assigned to tasks, influencing their allocation, *C**P**U* *U**s**a**g**e* *a**n**d* *M**e**m**o**r**y* *U**s**a**g**e* reflect the load on available computational resources, *N**e**t**w**o**r**k* *L**a**t**e**n**c**y* represents the time delay in task execution due to network constraints, and *T**a**s**k* *C**o**m**p**l**e**t**i**o**n* *T**i**m**e* measures how long tasks are taking to complete with the available resources. By representing the system’s state as a vector, SARSA can evaluate the performance of each action (resource allocation decision) in different contexts and learn the optimal policy for handling task scheduling and resource distribution.

An action  *A*[*t*]  in SARSA represents the allocation of resources to specific tasks at time *t*. In an RPA system, an action could involve decisions such as assigning a specific task to a particular resource (e.g., which CPU or memory unit to allocate), deciding when to start or delay a task based on available resources and task priorities, and adjusting the distribution of resources for ongoing tasks to ensure optimal processing time.

The equation of an action can be represented as

*A*[*t*] = \[*T**a**s**k* *t*[*i*]→*R**e**s**o**u**r**c**e* *r*[*j*], *S**t**a**r**t* *T**i**m**e* *o**f* *T**a**s**k* *t*[*i*]\] (9.20)

where *T**a**s**k* *t*[*i*]→ refers to a specific task in the system, *R**e**s**o**u**r**c**e* *r*[*j*] refers to the computational or network resource (e.g., CPU, memory, bandwidth), and *S**t**a**r**t* *T**i**m**e* *o**f* *T**a**s**k* *t*[*i*]  refers to when the task should start, ensuring efficient execution while minimizing delays.

The reward function  *R*(*S*[*t*],  *A*[*t*])  defines how the system evaluates the outcome of an action *A*[*t*] in state *S*[*t*]. The reward is typically a combination of factors such as task completion time, resource utilization, and latency. A well-designed reward function should reflect the objectives of efficient resource allocation in RPA systems, such as minimizing latency, maximizing throughput, and optimizing resource usage.

A possible reward function could be defined as

$\\begin{array}{l} {R\\left( {S\_{t},\\mspace{6mu} A\_{t}} \\right) = - \\left( {\\alpha\_{1} \\times Task\\mspace{6mu} Completion\\mspace{6mu} Time + \\alpha\_{2} \\times Latency - \\alpha\_{3} \\times} \\right.} \\\\ \\left. {Resource\\mspace{6mu} utilization} \\right) \\\\ \\end{array}$ (9.21)

where *T**a**s**k* *C**o**m**p**l**e**t**i**o**n* *T**i**m**e* is the time taken to process the task, with the aim of minimizing it. *L**a**t**e**n**c**y*  is the delay in processing a task, which should be minimized. *R**e**s**o**u**r**c**e* *U**t**i**l**i**z**a**t**i**o**n*  reflects how effectively the available resources are being used, with the goal of balancing utilization without overloading any resource. *α*[1], *α*[2], *α*[3] are weights that define the relative importance of each factor in the reward function.

By minimizing the task completion time and latency and maximizing resource utilization, SARSA learns a policy that optimally allocates resources to tasks, improving system efficiency.

Once the reward is calculated, the SARSA agent updates its action value function *Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*])← based on the feedback received. The update rule is:

*Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*])←*Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*])+*α*(*R*[*t*]+*γ**Q*(*S*[*t* + 1],*A*[*t* + 1])−*Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*])) (9.22)

 172. This equation reflects the agent’s learning process, and *Q*(*S*[*t*],*A*[*t*])← is the current value of taking action *A*[*t*] in state *S*[*t*]. *R*[*t*] is the immediate reward for taking action *A*[*t*]. *γ**Q*(*S*[*t* + 1],*A*[*t* + 1]) is the expected future reward from the next state *S*[*t* + 1], following action *A*[*t* + 1]. The agent updates its action value function to reflect the observed reward, which guides future decisions. An iterative process enables SARSA to derive the best resource allocation policies by updating the action over time according to the rewards received for improved system performance. Continuous learning and adaptation to the ongoing state of the system ensure that efficient allocation of resources occurs, thereby reducing task completion time and latency while increasing throughput optimization. Improved operational efficiency is the upshot of this optimization, making it a potent tool for optimizing RPA workflows in real-time environments.

*   **Tabu search**

Local neighborhood search is one kind of Tabu search. Every viable solution, *A* ∈ *Ω*, has a set of neighbors that correspond to it, *M*(*A*) ⊆ *Ω*, where *Ω* is a collection of possible solutions. An action is known as a “move” to *B*^′ performed from S to arrive at an outcome *A*^′ ∈ *M*(*A*). Even if doing so results in decline in the goal or fitness function, TS proceeds from a solution to its best acceptable neighbor. This is the non-greedy Tabu search action (refer Algorithm 1), which prevents from getting stuck in the local optimal state. The techniques being researched are labeled as “Tabu” or “forbidden” to avoid cycling and trapping in local minima. These recently visited alternatives are kept over iterations in a list called the Tabu list. Everything is always checked against the Tabu list before proceeding. If a move is discovered in the Tabu list, the technique proceeds to the next iteration; if not, the move is discarded. The Tabu status of a particular solution may be overridden when a set of conditions (such as an ambition level or criterion) is satisfied. To enhance the search, intensification and diversification tactics are sometimes used. In the first example, the search is focused more on the viable domain’s potential areas. In the second scenario, an effort is made to consider options throughout a large portion of the search field. [Table 9.1](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch009_tab_001) lists the parameters for the TS algorithm.

## 9.9 Operational Management

In RPA, operational management is concerned with making sure that automated processes are executed smoothly, monitored, and optimized to improve performance and efficiency. Dynamic danger detection and mapping is the first step, in which sophisticated sensors and AI-powered technologies keep an eye on operating settings, identify irregularities, and evaluate task performance instantly. State encoding and contextual analysis are used in the process, which uses sensor and system log data to evaluate workload allocation, performance indicators, and resource usage.

where *y*[*t*] and *ϵ*[*t*] represent the current value and random error, respectively, at time period *t*; these are referred to as the model parameters ⌀[*i*](*i*\=1,2,…,*p*) and ⌀[*j*](*j*\=1,2,…,*p*).

[Figure 9.2](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-002) shows the flow chart for using the ARIMA model to predict new data. Loading and analyzing data is the first step in determining the appropriate model parameters. Furthermore, preprocessing the data is necessary to eliminate noise, which could lead to an inaccurate prediction. If available, seasonality, AR, MA, and I can be used to obtain each parameter. Next, using these parameters, we build an ARIMA model and estimate error by comparing it to actual data. To be considered appropriate, the error in the model’s prediction must surpass a specific standard value of ∈. Conversely, if the error exceeds, the values of a parameter that can be adjusted through the use of a parameter algorithm for learning will need to be changed. Then, using the revised parameters, create a new ARIMA model and run another error estimate. Repeat this process until the specified condition is satisfied by the error value (*e**r**r**o**r* <  ∈ ). ARIMA is used to anticipate future network performance based on historical latency data, which is crucial for preserving the quality of immersive experiences, particularly in applications. ARIMA uses time-series data to predict  174. latency trends, allowing for proactive resource allocation to avoid possible delays. This prevents disturbances in real-time communications, resulting in a smoother and more responsive user experience. The model works by combining autoregression (AR), which uses past values to predict future points, differencing (I), which removes trends and makes the data stable, and moving average (MA), which accounts for previous forecast errors. In the context of immersive applications, proactive latency prediction enables real-time adjustments to network orchestration techniques such as dynamic bandwidth allocation, hence optimizing end-user quality of experience (QoE).

### 9.9.1 Whale Optimization Algorithm

The Whale Optimization Algorithm (WOA) is used to increase the clarity and performance of a model. The behavior of whales inspires the WOA. Their style of exploring is known as the “bubble-net feeding method.” Humpback whales use a hunting technique in which they capture their food at the water surface by trapping them in a bubble net. They form this network by swimming on a route shaped like the number “6.” The target prey or a near approximation of the optimal solution is currently the best candidate solution in WOA. The others will make an effort to  175. improve their position. Mathematically, the WOA imitates the collective movement of a swarm in the following manner:

*N* = |*F*.*K*^(\*)|*x*|−*K*|*x*||(9.24)

*K*(*x*+1) = *K*^(\*)(*x*+1) − *F*.*M*(9.25)

where *x* represents the current iteration, *K* represents the position vector, *K*^(\*) represents the position vector that coincides with the best solution obtained, and *G* and *F* represent coefficient vectors. *F* and *G* are defined as follows:

*G* = 2.*g*.*t* − *g*(9.26)

*F* = 2.*t*(9.27)

The variable *r* is randomly distributed throughout the range of \[0, 1\], whereas the variable *g* progressively decreases from 2 to 0 over each of the iteration.

The process consists of two distinct stages. The first phase, known as the Exploitation phase, involves surrounding a prey and using a spiral bubble-net assault approach. The second phase, known as the Exploration phase, entails randomly looking for prey. The whole method is entirely explained in Algorithm 1, and the block diagram of WOA is shown in [Figure 9.3](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-003).

The specifics of each stage are outlined in the following sections:

1.  **i. The Exploitation phase**

![Figure 9.3 Block diagram of the WOA mechanism. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure9_3.jpg)

Figure 9.3 Block diagram of the WOA mechanism. [Return to text.⏎](#ch09.xhtml#R_24032-0872-009-Figure-003)

 176. This phase is further divided into two methods:

*   ***Shrinking encircling mechanism***

This can be done by minimizing the value corresponding to Equation 9.4. It should be noted that the value of *f′* is randomly selected within the range of \[-g,g\].

*   **Spiral updating position**

The distance between a whale and its prey is calculated using this method. To replicate the helix-shaped movement, the following spiral equation is used:

*K*(*u*+1) = *N*^(*h*)*e*^(*b**h*).cos (2*π**h*) + *K*^(\*)(*u*) (9.28)

where *b* is a constant and *h* is a random integer in the interval \[−1,1\]. The spiral model and the shrinking encircling mechanism are believed to have a 50% chance of winning. The mathematical model for this is provided below:

$K\\left( {u + 1} \\right) = \\left\\{ \\begin{matrix} {K^{\*}\\left\\{ u \\right. - G.N,\\text{\\quad\\quad\\quad\\quad\\quad\\quad\\quad\\quad}d < 0.5} \\\\ {N^{h}e^{bh}.\\cos\\left\\{ {2\\pi h} \\right. + K^{\*}\\left\\{ u \\right.,\\mspace{6mu} d \\geq 0.5} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right.$ (9.29)

where *d* is a random number from uniform distribution.

2.  **The Exploration phase**

On the other hand, *F* employed random values within 1<*G*<−1 throughout the exploration phase to compel the agent to leave this site. This is mathematically expressed as follows:

*N* = |*F*.*K*[*r**a**n**d*]−*K*| (9.30)

*K*(*u*+1) = *K*[*r**a**n**d*] − *G*.*N* (9.31)

where *K*[*r**a**n**d*] is a whale selected at random from the existing population.

Furthermore, to ensure balanced resource consumption and enhanced system stability, multi-objective optimization frameworks give priority to important performance measures, including latency, throughput, and energy efficiency. Performance monitors and automated dashboards keep an eye on current procedures, allowing for real-time interventions to remedy inefficiencies or errors. This methodical approach facilitates scalability and improves operational agility, which makes it simpler to adjust to shifting company needs. Operational management in RPA places companies on the right track toward long-term development and digital transformation by providing increased productivity, cost savings, and dependability through continuous learning and improvement.

## 9.10 Results and Discussion

### 9.10.1 Comparative Analysis

A comparative study of the proposed resource allocation and scheduling scheme against well-known approaches is performed. The comparison is performed using the following methods: greedy resource allocation (GRA) \[[21](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref21)\] and genetic algorithm (GA) for task scheduling \[[22](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp009_ref22)\].

### 9.10.2 Task Completion Time (TCT)

Task completion time (TCT) is the time that a task takes from initiation to termination. TCT, in the context of resource allocation and scheduling, is very important because it significantly impacts the system’s efficiency and responsiveness, especially in real-time applications like RPA. It aims to minimize the time taken for the completion of tasks through the efficient management and allocation of resources according to changing task needs.

![Figure 9.4 Number of tasks vs task completion time (s). Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure9_4.jpg)

Figure 9.4 Number of tasks vs task completion time (s). [Return to text.⏎](#ch09.xhtml#R_24032-0872-009-Figure-004)

 178. [Figure 9.4](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-004) shows the relationship between the TCT and the number of tasks. The figure shows that all three methodologies have different levels of performance. GRA and GA are performing differently in the proposed methodology. On the X-axis, the number of tasks increases from 1 to 5, while the Y-axis shows the corresponding TCT values in seconds. As the number of tasks grows, the TCT for GRA remains the highest across all task counts, indicating that its static resource allocation approach becomes increasingly inefficient as the system scales. GA, with its evolutionary approach, performs better than GRA but still results in moderate TCT values.

In contrast, the proposed method consistently outperforms both GRA and GA, achieving the lowest TCT values across all scenarios. This highlights the dynamic, adaptive nature of the proposed method, which optimizes resource allocation in real time, minimizing delays and ensuring faster task execution significantly as the number of tasks increases. The graph clearly demonstrates that the proposed method offers the most efficient solution for task scheduling and resource allocation, particularly in environments with growing task complexity.

### 9.10.3 Resource Utilization (%)

Resource utilization is the efficiency with which a system uses its available resources, such as CPU, memory, bandwidth, and energy, during the execution of tasks. This is a crucial metric in the evaluation of effectiveness of strategies involving resource allocation, especially in dynamic contexts like RPA, cloud computing, or networked systems. Generally, high resource utilization suggests that the system is exploiting its  179. resources efficiently, whereas low utilization suggests wastage or utilization of the capacity. [Figure 9.5](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-005) shows the comparison of RU of the proposed allocation method with GRA and GA across various task counts. With the increase in the number of tasks, GRA demonstrates the maximum utilization by rising from 80% at one task to 98% at five tasks, thus exhibiting wasteful over-allocation. GA shows an average usage between 75% and 93%. The proposed allocation method has the lowest usage, from 70% to only 88%, which indicates its flexibility and effective use of resources. This will result in maximum system performance with minimal waste, making it the most effective approach.

![Figure 9.5 Number of tasks vs resource utilization (%). Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure9_5.jpg)

Figure 9.5 Number of tasks vs resource utilization (%). [Return to text.⏎](#ch09.xhtml#R_24032-0872-009-Figure-005)

### 9.10.4 Latency (ms)

Resource utilization refers to the extent to which the capacity of a system of available resources, including the central processing unit, memory, communication bandwidth, and energy, is fully exploited while performing tasks. It is a very significant measure within the analysis of performance of strategies that entail the utilization of resources, particularly within rapidly changing environments such as those encompassed by RPA, cloud computing or networks. Commonly, high resource utilization implies that the system is effectively utilizing the resources available to it, while low utilization implies that the capacity was either wasted or underutilized.

[Figure 9.6](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-006) shows the assessment of the performance of RU proposed based on the allocation method across different task counts using GRA and GA. Therefore, GRA  180. increases the maximum utilization waste over-allocation from a single task to five tasks, represented as 80% and 98%, respectively. The communication channel shows an average usage of 75 to 93% according to GA. Like elasticity, resource usage is the lowest for the proposed allocation method, ranging between 70% and 88%. It means achieving the highest capacity of the system with least loss possible, which makes it the most efficient strategy.

![Figure 9.6 Number of tasks vs latency (ms). Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure9_6.jpg)

Figure 9.6 Number of tasks vs latency (ms). [Return to text.⏎](#ch09.xhtml#R_24032-0872-009-Figure-006)

### 9.10.5 Throughput (s)

System throughput is defined as the number of data units or tasks that an existing system can complete in a specified time frame. Higher *A* value means better efficiency and performance of the system. [Figure 9.7](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-007) presents the comparison of the throughput results obtained with the proposed allocation method to those obtained using GRA and GA for different task values. GRA is always found to be the slowest, with throughput ranging from 0.5 to 1.7 s and thus confirms inefficiency in handling tasks. The throughput for the proposed allocation method is in the range of 0.7–2.1 s, which is better than that for GA, which allows for the leveraging of its optimization functions. The proposed allocation method yields the highest throughput at 0.9 s and 2.5 s for five tasks, further highlighting that this method is the most suitable for dynamic allocation of resources in a system. This goes to underscore its capacity to support the most elementarily sophisticated task processing within the shortest time possible.

![Figure 9.7Number of tasks vs throughput (s). Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure9_7.jpg)

Figure 9.7Number of tasks vs throughput (s). [Return to text.⏎](#ch09.xhtml#R_24032-0872-009-Figure-007)

 181.  

### 9.10.6 Energy Efficiency (J)

Energy density is a term that describes the level of work output or tasks accomplished with the energy used by a particular system of concern. It is a very significant parameter for assessing the effectiveness of the methods used in resource management and/or time slotting in energy-limited systems.

[Figure 9.8](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-008) shows energy efficiency for the proposed allocation method and benchmarks: GRA and GA for various task counts. GRA responds the least to energy efficiency, starting from 0.3 tasks/J and rising to 0.9 J, which is lagging in energy optimization. On the other hand, GA shows a moderate performance, and it converts energy efficiency ranging between 0.4 J and 1.1 J due to some evolutionary optimization. The proposed allocation method is the most energy-efficient – ranging from 0.6 J to 1.5 J for five tasks that demonstrate the method’s capability to coordinate task execution while saving energy smoothly; hence, it is efficient under conditions where power is a limitation and also under dynamic conditions.

## 9.11 Research Summary

In an attempt to enhance job scheduling, resource allocation, and performance monitoring in operational management, this study developed a theoretical framework of the Intelligent Operational Management Framework for RPA. The research enhances  182. the RPA systems by incorporating ML with reinforcement learning techniques, improving the overall performance while making the systems more scalable and less rigid. They identified key problems such as latency, resource consumption, and energy consumption and provided a reliable and scalable solution that can be applied across a range of businesses. The advantage of the proposed framework is proved by comparing current methodologies as, following its implementation, the achievement of higher performance indicators and adaptation to the process of digital transformation are ensured.

![Figure 9.8 Number of tasks vs energy efficiency (J). Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure9_8.jpg)

Figure 9.8 Number of tasks vs energy efficiency (J). [Return to text.⏎](#ch09.xhtml#R_24032-0872-009-Figure-008)

A comparison of the proposed method, GRA, and GA is shown in [Figure 9.9](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872-009-Figure-009) and [Table 9.2](#ch09.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch009_tab_002). The proposed method is shown to be effective in all measurements. It presents the shortest total time of tasks (≃12.35 s), suggesting that it has a quicker rate than GRA (≃19.75 s) and GA (≃16.58 s). The allocation of resources that are available in the resource-limited systems to the proposed method is optimal and the highest among all the methods being considered here, with a value of ≃94.52 %, followed by GRA with ≃80.34% and GA with ≃85.29%. Network latency is thus minimized (≃35.24 ms), which guarantees real-time response rate compared to GRA (≃58.65 ms) and GA (≃45.78 ms). The proposed method is also equally good in terms of throughput, which is ≃27.89 s compared to GRA ≃19.32 s and GA ≃22.45 s. Last but not least, it provides optimal energy efficiency of about ≃ 9.18 J, higher than that of GRA (≃12.34 J) and GA (≃10.76 J), indicating the generality and reliability of the proposed method in resource management and scheduling.

## 9.12 Conclusion

This chapter provides an intelligent operational management model of RPA to overcome the challenges accompanying achievement of resource optimization, low latency, and scalability of business process automation. The elaborated framework is based on sophisticated ML methods such as opti-filter XGBoost and APSO, which are used for feature selection and predictive accuracy. Furthermore, reinforcement learning, such as SARSA-TSA, is used for dynamic resource allocation and scheduling in the real-time environment, and ARIMA-WOA controls performance. The  184. performance indicators outline that the proposed method outperforms the current strategies in terms of reduced task completion time, efficient use of resources and energy, reduced latency, higher throughput, and better energy efficiency. This work shows the impact of combining intelligent optimization procedures to improve the scalability, flexibility, and functionality of RPA systems. Although this is described as a flexible solution applicable to a multitude of industries outside the banking sector, there is one more tool that aids companies in optimizing processes, reducing costs, and improving their performance to set the stage for further development in smart automation.

# 10 Intelligent Forecasting Models Based on Neural Systems for Smart Business Management

Hina Riaz and Mohan Prasad Sharma

DOI: [10.1201/9781003561873-10](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003561873-10)

## 10.1 Introduction

In the world of digital technology, organizations must contend with growing complexity and unpredictability. Always being a vital process, in the past three decades, human resource management (HRM) has undergone a significant evolution in both shape and function ([1](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref1)). Business executives are quickly preparing for the digital age, and machine learning (ML)-based artificial intelligence (AI) promises to alter the HR department on a variety of levels ([2](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref2)). Reliable forecasting is essential for efficient resource utilization and decision-making. The development of neural system-based models, in conjunction with AI, ML, and the Internet of Things (IoT), has transformed forecasting in intelligent business management. ‘’Artificial intelligence can swiftly retrieve the database, obtain information, resolve our queries correctly, and deliver the best solution immediately and logically while emulating the information process of human mind and reasoning’’([3](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref3)). Several competencies form workforce management and so their evaluation aims to establish intelligent workforce ([4](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref4)).

However, studies conducted in the past few years have shown a number of disadvantages of using stand-alone AI methods to solve real-world issues. The bulk and ambiguity of datasets, the intricacy of real-world issues, and unclear or inadequate data are the primary causes of restrictions. The deployment of these conventional single AI techniques is therefore declining according to studies on AI technique applications. Instead, hybrid systems – which are produced by combining two or more distinct AI techniques – are used ([5](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref5)). Many AI support systems such as artificial neural networks (ANNs), fuzzy sets, and intelligent decision systems are employed in a variety of contexts.

A hybrid approach that blends the advantages of fuzzy logic and neural networks, neuro-fuzzy systems, provide strong instruments for intelligent forecasting in astute business management.

In this chapter, we are going to discuss how ANNs combined with fuzzy sets forming neuro-fuzzy systems are able to handle real-world business scenarios that frequently involve complicated, unpredictable, and nonlinear data, which these systems  187. excel at handling. With previously unheard-of accuracy and versatility, these systems use the enormous processing power of ANNs to examine and forecast patterns.

## 10.2 Fuzzy-Set Systems

In 1965, Zadeh published the first paper on a novel way of characterizing non probabilistic uncertainties, which he called “fuzzy sets.” A class of items with a range of membership grades is called a fuzzy set. A membership (characteristic) function, which gives each object a membership grade between zero and one, defines such a collection. In the context of fuzzy sets, some properties of the concepts of inclusion, union, intersection, complement, relation, convexity, etc., are established. Applications of this theory can be found in many disciplines, for example, in AI, computer science, medicine, control engineering, decision theory, expert systems, logic, management science, operations research, pattern recognition, and robotics. Fuzzy logic is a rule-based algorithm which considers multiple factors and handles uncertainty and imprecision in the evaluation process by assigning degrees to each factor and carries out combined calculation based on the set of rules to determine, for example, employees’ performance index, which is not possible with traditional assessment methods.

## 10.3 Neuro-Fuzzy Systems (NFS)

According to Jang’s proposal in 1993, neuro-fuzzy systems are ANNs and fuzzy logic combinations in the field of AI ([6](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref6)). There are three types of NFS as explained by Nauck et al. and Vieira et al.

In a cooperative NFS, the neural network component is initially employed to identify fuzzy sets and/or fuzzy rules, and then the resulting fuzzy system is employed for decision-making.

Concurrent neural networks (NFS) operate in tandem with fuzzy systems, either by supplying data to the fuzzy system or by altering its result.

A hybrid NFS uses pattern scanning to adjust the parameters of a fuzzy system using a learning method modeled after the ANN methodology.

Among the three, hybrid NFS are successfully used to identify pragmatic problems. The main application domains of NFS during these years are prediction and forecasting, traffic control, human resource management and information systems, production and operations, finance, business planning, marketing and distribution, healthcare, etc.

## 10.4 Artificial Neural Network

The term “artificial neural network” (ANN) is defined as follows: “Given a collection of data reflecting the mapping, a computing system is able to procure, encode, and visualize the mapping from a multi modal domain of data to another” ([7](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref7)). According to Haykin ([8](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref8)), ANN is a dynamically parallel arrangement of simple processing units that can learn from its surroundings and preserve that knowledge in its connections.

ANNs employ algorithms that aim to do a neurologically analogous task, such as reading from experiences, extrapolating from comparable circumstances, and evaluating states where subpar outcomes have previously been attained ([9](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref9)). ANNs are  188. proved to have stronger pattern detection and learning skills ([10](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref10)). Organizations use ANNs to tackle challenges ranging from functional prediction and system modeling to pattern recognition engines and robust classifiers. ANNs can generalize and make conclusions based on vague and loose input data. The size and complexity of the problem are not necessarily connected to how well an ANN learns and approximates correlations between the input and the output ([11](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref11)).

![Figure 10.1 Representation of DNFS by combining the advantages of fuzzy systems and a DNN.Source: Talpur, N., Abdulkadir, S.J., Alhussian, H. et al., 2023. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure10_1.jpg)

## 10.5 Deep Neuro-Fuzzy Systems (DNFS)

A deep neuro-fuzzy system (DNFS) is a sophisticated hybridization idea that combines fuzzy logic and deep learning techniques, such as deep neural networks, to address a variety of challenging real-world issues requiring data with large dimensions as shown in [Figure 10.1](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872-010-Figure-001). The significance of deep learning approaches is increasing quickly in comparison to other contemporary methods because of their exceptional performance in a variety of applications, including social, medical, audio, and visual data ([12](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref12)). It implicitly captures complicated large-scale data structures by allowing multi-layer cognitive models to learn and understand data with multiple degrees of abstraction, mimicking brain perception and knowledge representation.

## 10.6 Different Neural Network-Based Models Facilitating Forecasting and Smart Business Management

### 10.6.1 Finance Forecasting

Financial engineering has been the most significant topic among academia and the financial industry after growing over the past few years from financial signal analysis to financial prediction techniques. Over the past two decades, a number of hybrid intelligent macroeconomic forecasting systems that combine fuzzy logic, neural networks, and genetic algorithms have been proposed. [Lee (2020)](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref13) developed a chaotic type-2 transient-fuzzy deep neuro-oscillatory network (CT2TFDNN) with retrograde signaling to anticipate global financial outcomes as shown in [Table 10.1](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch010_tab_001)  189.  190. ([13](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref13)). The DNFS method has been applied to e-commerce platforms, stock index prediction, and Bitcoin price prediction by other studies ([14](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref14)–[17](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref17)).

### 10.6.2 Human Resource Management Forecasting

Employee selection, training and development, performance appraisal, designing optimal benefit plan, employee retention and absenteeism prediction are various HRM functions where NFS are used for intelligent forecasting. Doctor et al. propose neuro-fuzzy information system to rank applicant resume to frame fair and consistent resume ranking policy ([19](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref19)). To forecast the success of possible candidates, neuro-fuzzy algorithms can examine past data on employee performance, their abilities, and other pertinent variables, and this is called “predictive modelling.” Neural network-based intelligent systems like ANFIS uses the linguistic factors as system inputs to offer a solution for the staff management issue to maximize a company’s work assignment decision-making process ([20](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref20)): NFIS in human resource retention ([21](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref21)), ANFIS in employee absenteeism prediction ([21](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref21)), and NFS in performance evaluation ([21](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref21)).

### 10.7 Production and Operations Forecasting

For effective resource allocation, inventory control, and customer satisfaction, forecasting is essential in production and operations. The complexity and unpredictability of these activities are frequently too much for conventional tactics to handle.  191. These restrictions are circumvented by neuro-fuzzy systems. For utilizing industry trends, seasonal fluctuations, and outside variables to forecast consumer demand neuro-fuzzy models increase accuracy in dynamic environments by capturing nonlinear correlations between variables. Optimization of stock levels to prevent shortages and reduce holding expenses is improved using neural learning to forecast inventory needs. A neuro-fuzzy and PSO-based system was created by Chatterjee et al. ([22](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref22)) as a part of a robot system that is controlled by voice instructions where the inputs were the user’s spoken orders and the outputs were the robot system’s crisp control signals. Hidden Markov Model-based automatic speech recognizers were developed to extract key instructions from a running sentence. This technology was used to control a mobile robot’s motion and navigation in the investigation. A neuro-fuzzy framework was then created by Palluat et al. (2006) to monitor a flexible manufacturing system. The framework consisted of a dynamic ANN detection tool and a neuro-fuzzy diagnosis tool. The monitoring aid system was adjusted using the learning capability of the system. Fuzzy characterization of each cause was provided by the system to account for the uncertainties in the maintenance knowledge ([5](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref5), [23](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref23)). Hsiao et al. ([24](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref24)) used Lyapunov’s direct method to construct an equilibrium criterion for nonlinear multiple time-delay coupled systems. The Takagi–Sugeno fuzzy controller was used in the authors’ methodical design to guarantee the stability of nonlinear multiple time-delay systems that are interdependent. To mitigate the impact of modeling mistakes between Takagi–Sugeno fuzzy models with multiple time delays and nonlinear multiple time-delay subsystems, a robust layout of fuzzy control using a model-based approach and Lyapunov’s direct method was proposed ([25](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref25)).

### 10.8 NFS in Business Plan Forecasting

Administrators may recreate outcomes under a variety of variables, including changes in policy or market volatility, using NFS’s ability to model “what-if” scenarios ([26](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref26)–[28](#ch10.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp010_ref28)).

NFS forecast possible results for the introduction of new products or market expansions or sales prediction using trade and prior information. In this field, AI techniques have been applied to business planning for tense and real-time decision difficulties, simplifying current information, reducing a surplus of data, and a variety of other issues. Similar to other AI methods, NFS was used to automate a number of business planning issues, particularly those involving highly uncertain decision-making. A few studies, shown in Table10.4, proposed different neural models for business forecasting.

## 10.9 Conclusion

This chapter briefly discussed the methodology which strengthens the applicability of NFS-based approaches tending toward expertise orientation. NFS have shown to  192.  193.  194. be an effective tool in a number of fields, such as HRM, corporate planning, manufacturing and operations, and finance and are capable of handling complicated, unpredictable, and nonlinear data by fusing the advantages of fuzzy logic with neural networks.

In the domain of finance, NFS models have been effectively used for portfolio enhancement, assessment of risks, and stock price forecasting. These models offer important insights for well-informed decision-making by identifying complex patterns and trends in financial data for analysis.

 195. NFS forecasting models in manufacturing and operations improve supply chain management, optimize production procedures, and increase quality control. These models help firms make data-driven decisions and boost productivity by evaluating both historical and current data.

With AI advancements, NFS has helped HRM forecasting and decision making in various HR functions like employee selection, training and development, performance appraisal, designing optimal benefit plan, employee retention, and talent management.

Business planning can be effectively facilitated by accurately predicting future scenarios, and businesses can develop effective strategies and allocate resources efficiently.

As fuzzy models like ANFIS, DNFS, and ANNs evolve, they are expected to bring further advancements, leading to even more innovative and effective applications. As a result, future research could focus on designing interpretable neuro-fuzzy systems that strike a balance between accuracy and interpretability. These systems are essential for many decision-oriented commercial applications where transparency and model interpretability are crucial.
