# 11 Customer Churn Prediction Using Machine Learning

Meghali Das, Purnima Pal, and Garima Srivastava

DOI: [10.1201/9781003561873-11](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003561873-11)

## 11.1 Introduction

A holistic approach to creating, maintaining, and enhancing persistent and devoted relationships with consumers is called Customer Relationship Management, or CRM ([Sibisi 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref33)). It is widely accepted and widely used in a variety of industries, including the retail industry, telecom, insurance, and banking. A services market experiences aggressive competition with different providers offering attractive deals. Customers have the freedom of choice to swap institutions with comparatively better offers which leads to a direct loss of revenue. Businesses are putting a lot of effort into using a variety of tactics to thrive in this cutthroat environment. To enhance revenue, the banking sector employs major approaches like acquiring new customers also termed customer acquisition, upselling current clientele, and prolonging customer retention popularly known as customer attrition. When comparing each of these methods and accounting for each approach’s profit earned on the investment (return on investment) reveals that the last approach, which is retaining existing customers is the most profitable ([Almestarihi et al. 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref1)). It signifies that keeping an existing customer is far less expensive than acquiring a new customer ([Gurung et al. 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref13)). It is also regarded as being much simpler than the upselling strategy.

Retaining current consumers is one of the most crucial aspects of subscription-based product expansion. The banking and insurance market is highly competitive due to the large number of government-regulated and also private providers providing comparable services ([Booth 2022](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref3)). It only takes one bad experience to lose a consumer permanently. Additionally, a company would incur significant financial loss and reputational damage if a large number of unhappy customers abruptly left the company. A recent survey by Invesp found that the cost of customer acquisition can be five to six times higher than that for customer retention ([Pandey and Shukla 2021](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref31)).

Churn, commonly known as “customer churn,” is the term used to describe the phenomenon whereby once-loyal customers of a bank stop purchasing its products ([Klepac et al. 2015](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref19)). The rate at which a financial institution loses customers over a specified duration is called the churn rate, sometimes referred to by the term customer attrition rate. One method of determining the churn rate is to calculate the percentage of lost customers during a specified period to the total population of fresh clients gained in the course of the same duration ([Wagh et al. 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref37)). This gives the  198. customers’ churn rate. As an example, if a bank in a certain month gained 50 new clients while 2 customers left the bank, the customer attrition rate would be 4%.

The churn rate is an important metric for assessing an enterprise’s profitability. One should be cautious not to underestimate the impact of even minor churn. In a financial organization, just a fraction of quarterly or monthly churn can pile up over time. An annual churn rate of around 6% is reached with a minimum churn rate of only 0.5% every month. High churning rates, low earnings, and high client acquisition expenses are interrelated ([Kaya 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref17)). In a bank, advertising charges refer to both acquisition and retention costs. Acquisition expense is the amount a company allocates to acquire new customers. Retention expenses, on the contrary, are the costs of retaining existing customers ([Farquhar 2005](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref10)). Due to human limits, it is extremely impossible to predict which consumers will leave and which will remain. As an outcome, the distribution of funds may be erroneous, causing an increased quantity of cash disbursed.

With growing rivalry in service industries, the threat of client churn grows tremendously. The primary reason for churn is discontent with the buyer’s support and service system. The primary causes of attrition in the banking industry include lack of personalized attention, less data safety, unexplained deduction of charges from the account of the customer, and unsatisfactory interest rates and rewards, among many others. Some simpler reasons can be customers changing location or changing their financial goals, economic downturns, or less user-friendly digital platforms.

One of the several ways that can make a business perform poorly is through imperfect customer accession. In this situation, customers are not provided with detailed instructions regarding the use of their purchase and the purpose it can fulfill. Also, poor communication can leave customers unable to provide reviews or receive their concerns answered straight away. Poor onboarding results occur when clients are not furnished with apparent guidelines on exactly how a product should be used and what it is capable of. Loyal consumers who do not get as many inducements as new customers could be of the opinion that they are not appraised as highly. Especially, in the banking industry, a large percentage of customers heavily rely on the brand name rather than the services provided. In such situations, customers might withdraw from a bank at the first instance of a bad experience ([Ganesh et al. 2000](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref11)). In the fast-paced digital world, people have easy access to social media platforms, networking, and review websites. This makes it difficult for any organization to hide any scandalous news under wraps. Any negative public news or a bad review from a dissatisfied client would result in high churn rates and less customer acquisition. A recent survey reported that approximately 65% are skeptical of retail news releases, 71% do not embrace funded social media announcements, 55% of consumers have ceased to trust outlets, and 69% reject TV commercials ([Surya et al. 2021](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref34)).

The key to solving this problem is estimating which consumers will likely churn. The primary goal is to predict churnable consumers and determine the rationale for quitting. This will help build strategies for retaining clients. The basic purpose of churn research is to recognize and predict churnable customers at the earliest ([Mozer et al. 2000](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref28)). This will assist in resolving the customer issues. This will help to meet the customer requirements and they will keep utilizing the bank’s services.

 199. Van den Poel and Burez ([Burez and Van Den Poel 2009](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref4)) have shown that two procedures are used to manage client churn: proactive and reactive. When a firm adopts a reactive strategy, it pauses until the consumer asks the firm to terminate their package agreement. In this circumstance, the corporation will incentivize the consumer to adjourn. When the organization takes a pre-emptive strategy, it attempts to distinguish consumers who are susceptible to churning ahead of time. The firm then gives extra incentives to prevent these clients from leaving. Being capable of forecasting whether a certain client is likely to churn is a significant extra source of revenue for any firm.

To keep existing clients, the finance trade has to identify the causes for churn, which can be determined using insights derived considering obtained data. Extensive research shows that ML innovation is particularly effective in predicting this situation. This approach is applied by learning from past data. ML is a technique for the analysis of data that uses programming to stem analytical prototypes. ML, which engages a set of rules that continuously acquire knowledge using data, enables systems to discover undetected trends with no requirement for being specifically told where to seek. The three major groups of ML approaches are supervised, semi-supervised, and unsupervised. Supervised learning is the ML problem of identifying unknown arrangements in datasets with labels. Unsupervised learning is a ML activity that identifies hidden patterns in data that have no labeling. Semi-supervised learning stands somewhere between supervised and unsupervised learning. It uses both types of records where a small set of labeled records is used for training and a set of unlabeled data for prediction ([Injadat et al. 2021](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref15)). Churn prediction is a binary classification model, which identifies churners from non-churners.

The retention of existing customers costs significantly less than adding new subscribers to the network ([Lu et al. 2021](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref24)). Customer churn is a direct loss of profit for the company. If knowledge about customer turnover is available ahead of time, suitable efforts can be made to give superior service to such consumers. Long-term consumers generate more money for the company because they are less reactive to minor changes. The handling of churning consumers is one of the most difficult and significant challenges that the banking sector faces in the present day. According to the latest research, the primary goal is to find profitable churn consumers by analyzing massive amounts of data from financial institutions. In practice, there are numerous limitations to employing present approaches, which confront numerous challenges and obstacles in addressing the present-day churning problem. During model development, many features with important information are overlooked in the phase of feature selection ([Li et al. 2018](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref23)). Most statistical approaches are employed in a wide range of domains, which leads to unsatisfactory outcomes from current predictive models. Another major issue with existing techniques is choosing an array of features. Every consumer, whether individual or collective, may migrate for a variety of reasons. A consumer might be classified as a churner regardless of the causes and factors that led to their leaving the bank. During the churning process, diverse patterns of behavior emerge, and they should not be treated uniformly. Some consumers may not depart as rapidly as others. The demand now is for a more comprehensive prediction framework capable of predicting client attrition in advance ([Wu et al. 2021](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref38)). This will be extremely helpful in providing robust plans for retention of  200. various groups of churners, such as various offers based on the churn variables for each group. Motivated by the facts and observations presented above, this study proposes a model for predicting churners using various ML algorithms.

This research aspires to build a robust prediction prototype for customer churn by exploiting advanced ML techniques to assist businesses in identifying customers at high risk and implementing retention strategies proactively. The main purpose of this work is to build a perfect and scalable customer churn prediction model. This model will analyze historical customer data to predict churn and provide actionable insights that will enable businesses to implement effective retention strategies and enhance customer satisfaction. The customer churn prediction model will:

*   Explore past customer data to find models and aspects aiding customer churn.
*   Predict the likelihood of churn for existing customers.
*   Provide actionable insights to help banking enterprises develop targeted retention strategies.

## 11.2 Case Study of the Banking Industry for Churn Prediction Without ML

Citibank is a popular private bank in India. Its approach to controlling client churn illustrates the importance of ML in effectively managing customer attrition. Initially, the bank was dependent on standard data analysis approaches to gain rudimentary insights into churn trends. However, these methodologies had very little capacity to anticipate which consumers were most likely to swap the bank and the cause for it. The prediction capacity of Citibank drastically improved by employing ML algorithms like decision trees and logistic regression ([Nosratabadi et al. 2020](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref29)). ML models helped the bank recognize risky clients by analyzing complex trends in service utilization and histories of transactions. There was a rapid decrease of around 15% in attrition rates. This demonstrates how prediction models can deliver more in-depth, practical insights than conventional methods.

Another popular financial institution in the United States, Bank of America, emphasizes the importance of implementing ML approaches for identifying probable churners. At the initial stage, Bank of America also employed traditional methods like creating customer segmentation based on service usage and account activity ([Kovács et al. 2021](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref20)). Although these methods gave some insight, the predictive accuracy was not very appreciable. The bank then implemented different ML models like clustering. This improved its ability to predict probable unhappy clients and devise customized retention policies. The ML-oriented method confirmed more explicit and vigorous strategies and an approximate 12% decrease in churn rates. ML models have strengthened the ability to foretell the probable churn, enabling them to design more efficacious retention plans than old traditional segmentation.

DBS Bank is a Singaporean bank that also implements the widely used concept of ML in churn prediction, making it flexible to plan effective policies for retaining customers ([Jameaba 2022](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref16)). The bank was able to overcome the concern of low  201. revenue by adopting ML techniques over conventional methods. DBS Bank used algorithms like neural networks and gradient boosting to comprehend the factors that affect churn. This technology assisted in examining complicated client data and forecasting churn with great precision, resulting in a reduction in churn rates by 18%. This example underscores the need for ML to process huge dimensions of data and detect intricate patterns that established techniques might overlook, leading to more effective and timely retention efforts.

FinSecure Bank, another popular Indian financial institution used customary methods for analyzing client data. The organization shifted from regular data analysis to ML models with the introduction of ML concepts. FinSecure Bank used models such as gradient boosting and RF to predict churn. It was now capable of decoding complex customer behavior and implementing proactive retention strategies, primarily data oriented. The bank was significantly able to reduce the churn rates by approximately 20% ([Miller and Toman 2016](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref27)).

These case studies emphasize the precision and efficiency of ML models in the financial market for predicting high-risk clients. Although classical methods provide elementary ideas, ML models help gather improved forecasting powers and practical recommendations that greatly enhance the client-holding effort. The capacity of ML to assess challenging data trends and generate instantaneous predictions shows its outstanding effectiveness in regulating customer churn effectively.

## 11.3 Literature Review

A hybrid neural network was developed by Chih-Fong Tsai ([Tsai and Lu 2009](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref35)). CRM data collected from a telecom company in the United States was used for this project. Two combined models were designed for churn prediction by merging self-organizing maps (SOM) and back-propagation artificial neural networks (ANNs). The hybrid models are ANN + ANN and SOM + ANN. In the first phase, data is reduced. The initial step in the two fusion models, in particular, is to achieve a reduction of data by removing ineffective training data. The outcome of this stage is used to build a prediction algorithm. The models are evaluated on one set of standard data and two sets of imprecise data. The findings show that the blended models perform better than the standard neural network technique. The ANN + ANN model achieved better performance accuracy than the SOM + ANN model.

A novel technique that employed boosting algorithms was proposed by Ning Lu ([Lu et al. 2014](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref25)). The clients are segmented into two sections based on the degree dispensed by the boosting algorithm which could efficiently predict the group of customers who have higher chances of exiting the company. Logistic regression is applied as a primary learner, and a model for predicting churn is designed for each segment. The findings showed that the boosting method makes prediction better than the logistic regression.

Ssu-Han Chen ([2016](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref5)) proposed a novel technique built on the gamma Cumulative SUM (CUSUM) chart, which tracks each client’s inter-arrival time (IAT) through the usage of a finite fusion model to design the reference of a visualization chart and decision scale and an ordered Bayesian structure that records customer diversity.  202. Recency is another variable included in IAT that measures time intervals. It is incorporated into the framework to monitor the latest state of authentication activity. Additionally, the submitted explanation includes a GUI for each client. The findings revealed that the gamma CUSUM chart has a 5% higher accuracy (ACC) than exponential CUSUM and an average time to signal (ATS) that is approximately two days longer than exponential CUSUM.

Lee et al. ([2011](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref22)) employed a partial least squares (PLS)-based approach on strongly correlated data points to design an accurate and lucid predictive model for churn forecasting. In addition to building a prediction model for accurately estimating client attrition, a fundamental but profitable churn advertising strategy was also built. The recommended approach allows executives in marketing to effectively and seamlessly sustain a suitable number of retainers in promotional campaigns.

Koen W. De Bock ([De Bock and Van Den Poel 2012](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref8)) also proposed an ensemble classifier that utilizes the concept of generalized additive models (GAMs), which is evaluated based on accuracy and accessibility together. The model uses the random subspace method, bagging, and semi-parametric GAMs as fundamental classifiers. The models were evaluated using datasets collected from 6 major real-life situations and it was concluded that the proposed model shows better precision than standard GAM and logistic regression.

M.A.H. Farquad ([Farquad et al. 2014](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref9)) developed a combinational strategy to reduce the disadvantages of standard SVM models. This technique includes three phases: SVM-recursive feature elimination (SVM-RFE) is exercised in the foremost stage to minimize the collection of features. The next phase uses fewer features to generate an SVM framework. In the final phase, a combination of Naïve Bayes’ and decision trees is designed (called NBTree). An unbalanced dataset is used which has 7% lost customers and 93% devoted clients.

Ver-braken et al. ([Verbraken et al. 2013](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref36)) presented an innovative performance indicator, the expected maximum profit criterion, correlating with the clients’ major goals. The suggested structure helps enterprises choose a classification technique that increases revenue and gives data regarding the customer segment to target for retention strategies.

An approach based on the SVM algorithm was implemented by Benlan He ([He et al. 2014](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref14)) for predicting customer churn. They employed a random selection of samples due to the unbalanced data and boosted the accuracy of the system. SVM builds a hyperplane in a high-dimensional or infinite-dimensional feature space for classification. The random sampling approach is used to regulate data distribution and minimize imbalance in data usually created by less percentage of churners than non-churners.

Koen W. De Bock ([De Bock and Poel 2011](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref7)) suggested two ensemble classifiers named Rotation Forest (Rotforest) and Rotation Boosting (RotBoost) with the concept of rotation for predicting churn. Ensemble classifiers integrate multiple classifiers into one amalgated model incorporating a merger rule for integrating each classifier outcome. Rotforest uses extraction of features with specific attributes used to train individual basis classifiers, whereas RotBoost merges Rotforest and AdaBoost. Realistic datasets of four different sectors are used. Rotforests performed better than RotBoost in AUC and RotBoost had a better precision than Rotforest.

 203.  

## 11.4 Dataset Description

[Figure 11.1](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-001) represents the basic step of developing a prediction system. In the first stage, the dataset is gathered from the concerned bank. This dataset consists of raw data from clients which will be used for training the model. The second phase includes preprocessing data where noisy data like missing values, outliers, etc., are removed. Feature selection is the third phase in which the features that are most effective in determining the target variable are considered as shown in [Figure 11.2](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-002). The correct model is selected for prediction in the fourth phase. The selected model is then trained using a subset of the dataset in the sixth phase and in the seventh phase, the remaining part of the dataset is used for testing the selected model. The commonly used ratio of training to testing is 7:3. The perfect model is expected to predict a new sample in the right category. This classification is based on data on which the model is trained. Finally, in the last phase, the results of the model are compared to some criterion or previous prediction to measure its efficiency.

The dataset collected from a bank in the United States contains records of around 10,000 clients with 13 different attributes gathering various information that should prove essential in predicting if a particular customer is leaving the bank or not. A description of the dataset is provided in [Table 11.1](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch011_tab_001).

Initial processing showed that the dataset does not contain any null values. However, it contains records with the customer’s age skewed to the higher side as shown in [Figure 11.3](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-003). For precision, the columns RowNumber, CustomerId, and Surname were excluded. On further analysis, the location of customers was found to be limited to “France,” Germany, and Spain. We therefore encoded geography into numerical codes. The gender column was also converted into binary values, as demonstrated in [Table 11.2](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch011_tab_002).

In the feature selection phase, we analyzed how different features like the location of the customer, their gender, whether the client uses a credit card, the number of products the client has purchased, and whether the customer is operating the bank account or their account remains dormant would affect the churn behavior of the customer. As derived from [Figure 11.4](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-004), it can be emphasized that clients who reside in Germany are more inclined toward churning. It could be a case of low interest rates in a particular country. Also, churning was exceptionally high among males, as evident from [Figure 11.5](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-005). [Figure 11.6](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-006) shows that a customer who uses a credit card is inclined  204.  205.  206. to churn, which can be attributed to high interest rates and other hidden charges. The chart shown in [Figure 11.7](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-007) helps businesses understand if customers with fewer or more products are more likely to churn. It can be concluded from [Figure 11.8](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-008) that customers whose account has remained dormant for a certain period have eventually left the bank.

## 11.5 Methodology

According to our research, the most widely used ML algorithms for churn prediction are logistic regression, Naïve Bayes classifier, RF, and SVM ([Lalwani et al. 2022](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref21)). In this work, we have used three ML algorithms, namely KNN, SVM, and RF as shown in [Figure 11.9](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-009).

### 11.5.1 SVM

A popular supervised learning concept called SVM is frequently employed for solving regression and classification problems. SVM separates data points of different groups even with the greatest error margin by calculating the most suitable hyperplane. This method greatly reduces the probability of wrong identification on testing data. In a 2D space, the separating plane is called a decision boundary and in upper dimensions, it is referred to as a plane. This hyperplane helps identify the category of a new entry ([Ramadevi and Das 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref32)). The feature points that are close to the hyperplane are termed support vectors, and they decide the placement and alignment of the hyperplane. SVM focuses on the concept of maximization of the gap between the separating plane and the nearest data points. The best group for new data can be identified in a model with the maximum margin.

This algorithm shows high accuracy when data is linearly separable since a well-defined decision boundary can be created. The kernel in SVM transforms inseparable data into a different space where the data can be effectively categorized. The mapping to different coordinate spaces can be completed computationally by selecting the appropriate kernel parameters. Kernel functions that are primarily used  208. are linear, polynomial, RBF, and sigmoid kernels. The linear kernel helps classify data that is linearly separable, and the RBF kernel works for nonlinear data because it is capable of transforming a set of input features into an infinite-dimensional space creating intricate decision boundaries. The second parameter which makes SVMs a powerful technique is the value of *C*. This parameter is used primarily for data that is noisy and has many outliers. In a scenario where some bad classifications might be acceptable, SVMs are employed. For noisy data, a small value of *C* is selected. This would build boundaries with a wider margin, thus reducing erroneous classification. On the contrary, a high *C* value reduces the margin and increases the percentage of misclassification which is useful only after data cleaning.

![Figure 11.9 Accuracy chart. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure11_9.jpg)

Figure 11.9 Accuracy chart. [Return to text.⏎](#ch11.xhtml#R_24032-0872-011-Figure-009)

The main benefit of SVMs is that it is efficient in a high dimension and employs a very small part of training data to build the decision boundary. SVMs are most suitable for problems where the number of records is less than the number of attributes. It also has several disadvantages. The outcome can be affected by selecting the kernel and calibration of other related variables. Also, it has been shown to be susceptible to outliers.

SVM is popularly used in some applications. It is broadly used in text categorization such as spam classification, image classification such as identifying objects in images, and in bioinformatics such as in identifying genes and proteins.

### 11.5.2 Random Forest

RF is considered a popular ensemble learning concept available in ML for regression and classification tasks. It builds multiple decision trees during the training phase. The final category in a classification task is determined after calculating the mode of the output of each tree. For regression, this is done by calculating the mean of the individual predictions. The idea of RF is established based on the concept of a bagging algorithm. According to this algorithm, a number of algorithms (decision trees in RF) can be trained using different fractions of the dataset, created using sampling  209. with replacement which builds variations between the trees. This makes RF stronger and less liable to overfitting than a single tree ([Barreñada et al. 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref2)). The uncertainty in the forest arises from the shuffled data points and the feature selection. A random set of attributes is selected at each node in a tree and then the best split is calculated. This randomness makes the idea of an ensemble comparatively effective by decreasing interdependence among the trees.

A significant advantage of RF is its ability to supervise large datasets with higher attributes without feature selection. It can capture convoluted interactions between features and is highly flexible in dealing with both numerical and categorical data. Additionally, RF is robust to overfitting, particularly in large datasets, because it averages multiple trees’ outputs, thereby smoothing out the noise and preventing the model from learning from noise in data. This makes it an excellent choice for various real-world applications where the data might be noisy or the relationships between features and the target variable might be intricate and not easily captured by a single decision tree.

RF also calculates a feature importance factor for comprehending the data. This parameter helps position the features according to their significance and decreases the impurity of each tree. This measure contributes to removing the features that are less important, and the feature importance metrics are often used for feature selection, where less important features are removed to streamline the model, improve accuracy, and decrease training time, by considering only the most important attributes. RF is also empowered to handle missing data by splitting with alternate features if values for a particular attribute are missing.

This algorithm has many disadvantages. The most important drawback of this algorithm is that it can be programmatically costly and consume high storage, particularly for a huge dataset and large number of trees. Secondly, it can be challenging to deduce the system as the number of trees increases. In contrast to a single decision tree, which can be visualized and understood without effort, a forest is difficult to interpret which makes decision-making difficult.

RF is one of the most widely accepted and extensively exploited algorithms in the field of ML as it provides adaptability, strength, and proficiency in handling varied data and densities. It is utilized in numerous fields, such as finance for calculating credit scores, healthcare for predicting different diseases, and marketing for the segmentation of customers. Concisely, RF is an extremely effectual assemblage learning procedure that influences the potential of numerous decision trees to generate a model with high accuracy and generalization, making it the most popular concept for researchers in ML.

### 11.5.3 KNN

KNN is an easy-to-implement and efficient algorithm employed in regression and classification. It is a supervised learning algorithm used in ML. It is grounded on the outline that analogous data spots are expected to have related results, exploiting nearness in feature areas for making predictions. The KNN algorithm works on the rule of majority voting for classification. It uses averaging for regression. For  210. the latest data feature, KNN recognizes the “k” closest neighbors from the training dataset ([Ma et al. 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref26)). “k” is a variable used to decide the number of neighbors to be considered and then forecast the category of the new point calculating the class with maximum voting amid the neighbors or the average of the values. The distance is calculated with Euclidean, Minkowski, or Manhattan distance depending on a particular problem or the type of records.

The main advantage of KNN is its simplicity and ease of operation. This model does not have a training phase but saves all the training data and executes computations only for classification. The KNN is called a “lazy learner” or instance-based learning method, where the complete dataset is applied as the pattern. This algorithm does not make any guesses because it is non-parametric. This makes KNN very accommodating, and it can be used to solve a vast number of problems. This flexibility makes KNN to be specifically efficient under conditions where the decision boundary is asymmetrical or where the association between attributes and labels is complicated and nonlinear.

The flexibility of KNN also has many drawbacks. The greatest shortcoming is its programmatic ineffectiveness, mainly with huge datasets. As this model involves estimating the space among the latest data features and all other instances in the training dataset, the prediction phase can get lengthy as the dataset increases. This concern is aggravated in high-dimensional spaces. With the increase in the number of features, the gap between data points becomes less definite which makes it difficult for the model to find the nearest neighbors correctly. Scaling of features is important to confirm that all attributes participate evenly in the distance estimate process. This issue can be diminished by first applying normalization.

Another drawback of KNN is selecting the best value for “k,” the number of neighbors counted. A small value of “k” makes the model susceptible to noise, heading to overfitting and a large value of “k” can lead to underfitting. This issue can be solved by cross-validation which is used to find the best value of “k” for any known problem. Furthermore, KNN is responsive to the preference of distance parameter and the accuracy of the algorithm can fluctuate considerably subject to the method used. Regardless of these drawbacks, the flexibility and simplicity of KNN recognize it as a suitable standard model in many ML tasks. It is mainly effective in instances where the dataset is trivial, simple, and well-structured.

KNN is applied in numerous fields like pattern recognition, image processing, and data mining. For instance, it is usually used in anomaly detection, recommendation systems, and medical diagnosis. In a recommendation system, KNN is used to propose articles similar to those a client has previously liked, centered on the preferences of similar clients. Anomaly detection uses KNN to recognize outliers by relating the distances between concerned data points and their nearest neighbors. KNN is also used in medical diagnosis to categorize ailments based on patient indications by associating them with earlier history.

## 11.6 Results

After the initial stage of preprocessing and feature analysis, we performed a set of experiments for three different classifiers. In this chapter, we used three ML  211. algorithms such as KNN, decision tree, and RF. The dataset is separated into a training dataset and a testing dataset in the proportion of 7:3. The performance of the models is evaluated based on precision, accuracy, F1-score, and recall metrics. These four metrics are primarily dependent on the four factors, namely True Positive, True Negative, False Positive, and False Negative. These four factors in the context of churn prediction are described in [Table 11.3](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch011_tab_003).

These measures when represented in a 4 X 4 matrix are called a confusion matrix as shown in [Table 11.4](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_ch011_tab_004).

For churn prediction, the number of FN should be less than the number of FP. The reason behind this is that if an unhappy customer is not identified on time, the banking service will not be able to take preventive measures ([Burez and Van Den Poel 2009](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref4)). Additionally, the churned customers will not only ensue a substantial deficit of revenue but also affect customer acquisition in the future as the banking services heavily rely on peer reviews. At the same time, the number of FP will incur unnecessary costs to the enterprise for planning retention strategies for customers who do not need it as shown in [Figure 11.5](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872-011-Figure-005).

The sum of TP and TN represents the number of clients identified correctly and the sum of FN and FP depicts the number of clients identified incorrectly. The metrics for evaluating the attainment of a ML model can be calculated using the confusion matrix and equations (11.1)–(11.4) ([Das and Srivastava 2023](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref6)).

**Accuracy:** It is used to calculate the total number of correct predictions. The RF model achieves the highest accuracy at ~86%. KNN and decision trees achieve an accuracy of ~76% and ~79%.

Accuracy = (T[i]+T[j])/(T[i]+F[j]+T[j]+F[i]) (11.1)

| False Positive (FP) || Number of customers who were predicted as churners incorrectly |

**Precision:** It is the percentage of TP.

Precision = T[i]/(T[i]+F[j]) (11.2)

**Recall:** It describes the number of TP that were correctly predicted.

Recall = T[i]/(T[i]+F[i]) (11.3)

**F1-Score (F1):** It is the harmonic mean of recall and precision particularly used for unbalanced distribution in classes.

F1-score = 2T[i]/(2T[i]+F[j]+F[i]) (11.4)

## 11.7 Conclusion

In recent years, development progress has been showing the most sweeping bang ever. Technology innovation brings an expansion in services, and it is tricky for any enterprise to envisage the buyers who are prone to quitting the company. Consumers can be convinced to be retained and prevented from switching to a rival company if they are offered a reliable prototype of how the company plans to earn revenue and expand its operations in the future.

The churn prediction in the finance sector has garnered attention of many scholars in the present age of technology ([García et al. 2017](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref12)). In this study, we postulate a relative analysis of customer churn categorization in the banking business exploiting popular ML techniques such as RF, decision trees, and KNN. The experimental findings indicate that RF, an ensemble learning technique, achieves the maximum precision among all three algorithms, recording a score of 86%. RF outpaced decision trees and KNN algorithms in all performance metrics such as precision, recall, and F1-score. Also in existing research, we found that numerous ensemble learning methods have managed to surpass the traditional ML algorithms ([Owolabi et al. 2024](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref30)). Predicting which customers are likely to switch to other enterprises in the same market for better deals proves to be very monotonous. The rising trend of start-ups has made the market severely competitive both for existing and upcoming businesses. It requires a lot of research and analysis to predict clients who plan to switch to the parent company and come up with innovative strategies and deals for retaining these customers without making a hole in the pocket of stakeholders.  213. Therefore, enterprises aim to consider the most high-risk customers and present them with offers better than what their rival company advertises. It is very tough to predict authentic purchasers of services. In the upcoming days, with the advancement in the concepts and structures in the domain of deep learning and reinforcement learning, ML is attesting to be one of the most competent approaches to attend to issues like churn prediction with improved precision and accuracy ([Kejriwal 2023](#ch11.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp011_ref18)). In our study, we plan to build a more robust and flexible system utilizing the advanced frameworks of deep learning. It is likely that in the near future these statistics will be useful to calculate churn, which could be of huge support. It is essential to frequently enhance prediction models to enable executives to design fruitful, efficient, and flexible strategies to attract new customers and retain the existing ones.

# 12 Intelligent TechniquesFuzzy Logic and Computational Techniques

Swarnima and Bhawna Khullar

DOI: [10.1201/9781003561873-12](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003561873-12)

## 12.1 Introduction

Artificial neural networks (ANNs) \[[1](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref1)\], fuzzy logic, and genetic algorithms (GAs) \[[2](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref2), [3](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref3)\] are examples of computational intelligence approaches that are prominent study topics because they can handle complicated engineering issues that are challenging for traditional methods to tackle \[[4](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref4)\]. There has been a lot of interest in hybrid techniques in the field of computational intelligence. One of the most widely used strategies is the hybridisation of fuzzy logic and GAs, which results in fuzzy evolutionary algorithms \[[5](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref5)\] and genetic fuzzy systems (GFSs) \[[6](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref6)\]. Both are well-known instances of how soft computing approaches can work together constructively. In essence, a GFS is a fuzzy rule-based system (FRBS) enhanced by an evolutionary computation-based learning process that incorporates several evolutionary algorithms (EAs), such as genetic programming, GAs, and evolution strategies \[[7](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref7)\]. With only a performance indicator, GAs – a popular and common global search method – can comb through an extensive search space for appropriate answers. The computer development of a FRBS can be thought of as an identification or efficiency problem. Given their generic code structure, distinct performance features, and ability to find nearly optimal solutions in challenging search environments, GAs are excellent choices for prior information. In the setting of FRBSs, this previous knowledge could be represented as linguistic variables, fuzzy rules, fuzzy function of membership parameters, etc. Over the past few years, these characteristics have expanded the usage of GAs in the creation of numerous methods for designing FRBSs. Later in the year 1988, a guest editorial was written by Goldberg and Holland \[[8](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref8)\] that describes the utilization of GAs and Machine learning (ML). In the same editorial, they have also explained the GAs and classifier systems with a significant set of references. FRBSs offer a tool that can transform an expert knowledge-based language control approach into an automatic control strategy. They work especially well for simulating the correlation between variables in settings that are either unclear or extremely complicated. One such complicated issue that can benefit from the application of FRBS is the adaptation of GA parameters, which results in what are known as fuzzy adaptive genetic algorithms (FAGAs). The term fuzzy EAs can be applied to any type of EA that can be enhanced using methods based on fuzzy logic. This chapter provides a brief overview of the state of the subject, presents GFSs and fuzzy EAs, and highlights some of the more intriguing recent developments and potential directions.

 217.  

## 12.2 Systems Based on Fuzzy Rules

Building FRBSs is one of fuzzy logic’s primary accomplishments. These systems are based on the basic concepts of fuzzy IF-THEN rules and language parameters. A common example of a fuzzy IF-THEN rule is “If the climate outside was high, then the velocity of the fan ought to be high.” By simulating human thinking, FRBSs are able to handle these fuzzy rules and depict dynamic, complicated systems, many of those that are by nature uncertain rather than exact. Because of this, FRBSs can demonstrate a high degree of resilience to shocks, changes in system parameters, and other variables. Using a rule induction technique, fuzzy rules for a FRBS may be produced using data or expert knowledge. In this section, we go over the key components of FRBSs and provide a brief introduction to various fundamental ideas, such as fuzzy sets and variables that are linguistic.

### 12.2.1 Initial Steps: Linguistic Variable and Fuzzy Set

Unlike a sharp set, an ambiguous set permits its constituents to have various degrees of belonging. The belonging function, a line or a curve that illustrates the link among what is included in the set’s scope and its level of affiliation, is the essential part of a fuzzy set. An element *x*’s membership to a fuzzy set *A*, represented as 𝜇𝐴(𝑥) or simply 𝐴(𝑥), can range from 0 (indicating complete non-membership) to 1 (indicating full membership), meaning it can take any value within the interval \[0,1\] in accordance with Zadeh’s original concept \[[9](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref9)\]. An extension of the concept of a set in which the function of membership can only accept values between 0 and 1 is a fuzzy set. The amount that *x* helps generate *A* is shown by a metric of 𝐴(𝑥). Linguistic variables are phrases or phrases in an automatic or computerised language rather than numerical values. It is evident that this idea was created as an analogue of the idea of a variable with numbers, in particular, a variable in language. According to Ref. \[[10](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref10)\], L is a quintuple: X is the domain (world of discourse) of the base variable, A = {A1, A2, …, AN} is the set of linguistic terms of L (referred to as the term-set), g is a syntactic rule for producing linguistic terms, and m is a semantic rule which assigns a fuzzy set in X to each linguistic term. Three linguistic expressions, “Low, Medium, and High,” are used in [Figure 12.1](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872-012-Figure-001) to depict a situation involving the linguistic variable Temperature. The temperature, conveyed through the proper physical units, is a fundamental variable.

### 12.2.2 Fundamental Components of FRBSs

The main building block of a fuzzy FRBS is a collection of IF-THEN rules, with fuzzy assertions serving as the prior data and consequents connected by fuzzy deductive and inference compositional rules. The knowledge base (KB) of a FRBS displays information as rules that are fuzzy with IF-THEN conditions.

*   IF, certain requirements are met;
*   THEN, a series of conclusions can be drawn.

![Figure 12.1 Three linguistic statements and an example of the linguistic variable Temperature. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure12_1.jpg)

Figure 12.1 Three linguistic statements and an example of the linguistic variable Temperature. [Return to text.⏎](#ch12.xhtml#R_24032-0872-012-Figure-001)

 218. A module for the inference engine comprises the following:

A user interface for fuzzing, which creates fuzzy groupings out of clear data.

An inference system that uses a reasoning technique to draw conclusions using them in conjunction with the KB.

A defuzzification interface that uses a defuzzification technique to convert the fuzzy rule action that is so derived into a real action.

In general, FRBSs fall within one of many groups:

> The first is language modelling using groups of IF-THEN rules with linguistic values as antecedent. The behaviour of the system can be explained using natural language.

A rule structure characterized by function consequents and fuzzy antecedents forms the basis of the second category, which primarily focuses on scattering division or approximation. In contrast to conventional linguistic models, FRBSs are another kind of fuzzy model that makes direct use of fuzzy variables \[[11](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref11)–[13](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref13)\]. A database (DB) and a rule base (RB) make up the knowledge base (KB) in linguistic FRBSs. A DB contains the collection of linguistic terms considered in the rules of linguistics  219. and the membership procedures that define the function of the linguistics labels. A rule connective (or “also” operator) joins a group of linguistic rules to form a RB. To put it another way, several rules may execute for the exact same input at the same time. [Figure 12.2](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872-012-Figure-002) displays generic structure of an FRBS.

![Figure 12.2 Structure of an FRBS. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure12_2.jpg)

Figure 12.2 Structure of an FRBS. [Return to text.⏎](#ch12.xhtml#R_24032-0872-012-Figure-002)

## 12.3 Genetic Fuzzy Systems

By employing “IF-THEN” rules, which specify the antecedents and consequents using fuzzy logic statements rather than classical ones, FRBSs expand on conventional rule-based systems. Numerous applications such as control issues \[[14](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref14)\], modelling \[[15](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref15)\], classification, and data mining \[[16](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref16)–[18](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref18)\] have shown their efficacy.

In essence, a GFS is a fuzzy system enhanced by an evolutionary computation-based learning process that incorporates, among other EAs, GAs, programming languages, and evolution strategies. This concept is shown in [Figure 12.3](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872-012-Figure-003), where various FRBS components are learnt or adjusted by the genetic process. The ability to frame the design of the KB as an optimisation problem is essential for using a GA for self-learning of an FRBS.

Finding the right KB is comparable to framing it as a set of parameters and figuring out which parameter values produce the optimum performance based on the  220. fitness function from an optimisation perspective. The search space is provided by the KB parameters and is altered according to a genetic representation. Therefore, determining the particular KB components that the GA can optimise is the first step in creating a GFS.

![Figure 12.3 Genetic fuzzy systems. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure12_3.jpg)

Figure 12.3 Genetic fuzzy systems. [Return to text.⏎](#ch12.xhtml#R_24032-0872-012-Figure-003)

## 12.4 Taxonomy of Genetic Fuzzy Systems

By placing a case study in a chromosome, the design process may be viewed as a search problem inside the framework of design, which is a collection of rules. This is the basic idea underlying autonomously learning FRBSs by employing GAs. The two stages of GFS approaches are learning and tuning. However, it is difficult to make a clear distinction between these two processes, since drawing a distinct line between them becomes as difficult as defining the concept of learning itself \[[19](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref19), [20](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref20)\].

**Genetic tuning:** We employ a genetic tuning process to improve the FRBS performance in the presence of a KB without changing the existing RB. In other words, to improve performance by changing the FRBS settings while keeping the same RB.

**Genetic learning:** The second technique is to learn KB components, where a flexible algorithm for inference may even be employed. Regarding the inspection of KB components alongside other FRBS components, we group the suggestions based  221. on these two procedures and the FRBS elements that go into the genetic learning process. Thus, we examine the taxonomy depicted in [Figure 12.3](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872-012-Figure-003) \[[21](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref21)–[23](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref23)\].

### 12.5 Current GFS Research Trends

In this section, we concentrate on six contemporary trends, which are of great interest at present and have a lot of promise for the near future, drawn from the extensive body of published GFS research.

Evolutionary multiobjective learning of FRBSs: It addresses the trade-off between precision and interpretability. Multiobjective evolutionary algorithms (MOEAs) are among the most researched subjects in computational biology because of their ability to identify a set of non-dominated solutions based on population methods in a single run. NSGA-II \[[24](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref24)\] and SPEA2 \[[25](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref25), [26](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref26)\] are two popular and widely utilised algorithms among the several MOEAs that have been suggested in the literature \[[27](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref27), [28](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref28)\]. When creating genetic learning algorithms for multiobjective GFSs as shown in [Figure 12.4](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872-012-Figure-004), it’s critical to strike a compromise between the learning mechanism’s interpretability and accuracy. In this procedure, the goals of accuracy and complexity/interpretability should be taken into account.

![Figure 12.4 Taxonomy of GFS. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure12_4.jpg)

Figure 12.4 Taxonomy of GFS. [Return to text.⏎](#ch12.xhtml#R_24032-0872-012-Figure-004)

** 222. GA-based techniques for new data mining techniques and fuzzy association rule mining:** In fields like data mining (DM)/knowledge discovery (KD), where information must be extracted from systems and presented in an intelligible way, or in systems for decision support, where the logic must be evident, comprehensibility of knowledge is crucial. Many research investigations have examined how language and terminology are used during the discovery process. Overall, language variables and phrases can greatly speed up the formulation of rules for associations and data inquiry, making it easier to see patterns and establish connections. However, in DM/KD tasks, the use of GAs and EAs for dynamic pattern association and rule extraction is common \[[29](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref29)\]. With a clear advantage over previous methods – its understanding in terms of fuzzy IF-THEN rules – the combination in the GFS sector offers fresh and practical tools for analysis of patterns and for extracting novel kinds of relevant information. In Refs \[[30](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref30), [31](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref31)\], we find intriguing contemporary studies devoted to the genetic extract of fuzzy rules of association. Future contributions on the application of GFSs for association analysis would be intriguing, concentrating on various research issues that arise in the field of frequent pattern mining \[[32](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref32)\].

**Learning genetic models based on low-quality data (noise data and vague data):** There are numerous real-world issues where learning models from ambiguous data are necessary. Researchers are not actively examining the experimental designs of GFSs that learn from data that is observed imprecisely. However, from the perspective of fuzzy statistics, fuzzy sets are primarily used to treat ambiguous data in classification and modelling problems. We may examine how well these classifiers perform on the kinds of tasks where fuzzy systems are anticipated to perform better by using ambiguous data to train and test GFSs. Initial findings in this field include the analysis of fitness functions (with fuzzy values) developed in the context of GFSs \[[33](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref33)\] and the development of several formalisations for the definition of fuzzy classifiers based on the interactions between random sets and fuzzy sets \[[34](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref34)\]. It would be worthwhile to investigate this new field in the near future as it could yield intriguing findings.

**Context adaptation and fuzzy partition genetic learning:** The DB learning includes defining the fuzzy function of membership associated with each label, defining the total amount of labels for every linguistic factor and specifying the discourse universes. The effects of fuzzy partition granularity on FRBS performance were examined in Ref. \[[35](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref35)\]. Proving that using a suitable number of phrases for each language parameter can substantially improve the FRBS reliability without requiring a complex RB learning mechanism. Conversely, the idea of adding context to fuzzy systems arises from the realisation that, in reality, the same fundamental idea might be interpreted differently depending on the circumstances. Various methods have been put out to address nonlinear situations employing GAs, granularity, membership function learning, etc. \[[36](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref36)–[38](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref38)\].

**Components of inference engines that have undergone genetic modification:** We are aware that parametric aggregation operators can be used to try to find the best parameter configuration for any application by designing the inference system and the defuzzification technique. To obtain more precise fuzzy models, these components’ adjustment may be taken into consideration. To determine the best settings, we have encountered many GFS methodologies.

 223. This is an interesting field of research that could provide us with the opportunity to adjust the inference settings to fit an FRBS and develop neural networks that can coevolve the inference engine constants and the KB components.

**Revisiting the Michigan-style GFSs:** In Ref. \[[39](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref39)\], the first Michigan-style GFS was described. The concept of “rule strength” forms the foundation of all early methods in this field. A rule (classifier) gains “strength” through interactions with its environment and is rewarded or penalised according to how well it performs. This strength helps resolve disputes when many rules match at the same time and aids in GA’s fitness evaluation during learning episodes. An alternative method is to evaluate a rule’s appropriateness by its “accuracy,” not its strength. Casillas et al. presented a novel method for obtaining accuracy-based Michigan-style GFSs in their work. Derived from XCS and specifically tailored for fuzzy systems, the Fuzzy-XCS framework shows promise in online learning problems related to function approximation and robot simulation. The UCS algorithm was also extended using a new Michigan-style genetic learning approach for categorisation. These methods create a link between fuzzy system models and genetic learning research in the Michigan style. This is a fascinating area of study that may yield intriguing findings soon.

## 12.6 Fuzzy Evolutionary Algorithms

To improve the performance of these search algorithms, there is currently a growing interest in modelling various EA components or adjusting EA control parameters using fuzzy tools and fuzzy logic-based methodologies. EAs produced by this integration are typically referred to as fuzzy EAs.

### 12.6.1 Fuzzy Adaptive GAs

A specific type of FRBS called fuzzy logic controllers (FLCs) offers a tool that can transform a linguistic control strategy based on expert knowledge into an automatic control strategy. They work especially well for simulating the interaction between variables in situations that are either highly complicated or poorly defined. One such complicated issue that FLCs may help with is the adaptation of GA parameters, which results in what are known as fuzzy adaptive GAs (FAGAs). A wide variety of adaptive strategies for GAs can be captured and represented more easily by the rule bases of FLCs (for instance, they might assist the automatic learning of such strategies). The fundamental concept behind FAGAs is the employment of an FLC with GA control parameters as its outputs and any combination of GA performance measurements or current control parameters as its inputs. The FLC receives the GA’s current performance metrics and uses them to calculate new control parameter values that the GA will employ. [Figure 12.5](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872-012-Figure-005) illustrates this procedure.

## 12.7 Designing FAGAs

An outline of the difficulties in designing a FLC that a fuzzy adaptive genetic algorithm (FAGA) uses is provided in this section. Choosing the right inputs and outputs,  224. establishing the related database, and creating the rule basis are some of these difficulties.

![Figure 12.5 The FAGA model. Return to text.⏎](Images/Figure12_5.jpg)

Figure 12.5 The FAGA model. [Return to text.⏎](#ch12.xhtml#R_24032-0872-012-Figure-005)

*   **Inputs:** They ought to be reliable metrics that explain GA behaviour and the impact of genetic operators and genetic configuration factors. Diversity measurements, maximum, average, and minimum fitness, among others, are examples of potential inputs. One might also think of the existing control parameters as inputs.
*   **Outputs:** They show the control parameters’ values or the evolution of those values. Several outputs were described, including population size, crossover probability, mutation probability, selective pressure, the amount of time the controller must remain in a target state for the process to be deemed successful, and the extent to which the most effective answer has been reached.
*   **Database:** Each input and result should be given a set of language labels. The fuzzy division in a database, which can be expressed by the fuzzy array of membership operates defines the literal significance of these labels. Each input and output must have a restricted range to build these membership functions over a specified range of values.
*   **Rule Base:** After selecting the inputs and creating the database, the ambiguous regulations that explain the connections that exist must be defined. They make it easier to record and depict a wide variety of GA adaptation techniques. Although rule bases can be derived from the expertise and experience of GA specialists, many writers have encountered challenges in this process.

 225.  

## 12.8 Fuzzy Representations

A fuzzy technique is proposed to solve parameter optimisation issues with variables on continuous domains. The range \[0,1\] contains several (*m*) fuzzy alternatives for every issue parameter. The values of the variables that were selected for each parameter are subsequently combined to form the chromosomes. In the decoding process, each parameter is linearly adapted from the interval of interest \[0,1\] to the suitable characteristic field and an operator 𝑔: \[0,1\] 𝑚 → 0 and 1 g: \[0,1\] *m* → \[0,1\]. An illustration of such a function, as supplied by the authors, is shown below:

∀d = (d1,…,d*m*) ∈ \[0,1\] *m*, g(d) = 2*m* – 11 − *m*1j = 1∑*m*dj2j − 1 (12.1)

This kind of encoding goes against the core premise of classical evolutionary algorithms (EAs) that genotype and phenotype match one to one when *m*\>1, as two different genotypes can produce the same phenotype. Consequently, the mapping between genotype and phenotype is not isomorphic, meaning that the phenotype cannot be simply inferred from the genotype. A number of experiments using 𝑚 = 1 and 𝑚 = 2 were carried out in 2014 to show that employing a fuzzy representation can result in resilient behaviour. It outperformed the Breeder GA in certain instances. Another significant finding was that, for small population sizes, 𝑚 = 2 performed marginally better than 𝑚 = 1, but for greater population sizes, the opposite was true.

Specifically, the following equation specifies each search parameter:

*p**s* = (*p**g*.2[*l*]−1).*R* + *O* (12.2)

where R stands for a certain range of parameters, O for an offset, l for the number of bits in the genetic parameter, ps for the search parameter, and pg for the genetic parameter. To get more accurate answers, the same number of binary bits can be used by changing the offset and range.

## 12.9 Upcoming Research on Fuzzy EAs

The following concerns might be considered in future studies to create successful FAGAs.

*   **Important Data for FLCs:** More research should be done on identifying pertinent input variables for FLCs governing GA behaviour. These factors ought to characterise either population conditions or chromosomal characteristics so that control settings can be modified accordingly to bring about noticeable performance gains. Accordingly, Boulif and Karim \[[18](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref18)\] recently asserted that earlier studies on FAGAs made it possible to create FAGA systems that perform noticeably better than ordinary GAs. Though intriguing, these publications focus on the consequences rather than the causes that drive the GA’s quest for sound answers. In fact, the population diversity, average fitness, and convergence speed are the inputs used by fuzzy models.
*    226. **Coevolution-Based Adaptation with Fuzzy Behaviours:** Two significant features make the modification of GA settings in cooperation with fuzzy behaviours (FBs), a viable approach for further FAGA research: (1) There hasn’t been much focus on using methods of learning online for creating FAGA rule bases and (2) coevolution-based EA adaptation is currently a popular field of study. Although the exact fuzzy rules connecting these features to the operator’s control actions are still unclear, the flexible system coevolved with FBs can also be used to solve situations where specific parent features may improve the efficacy of the crossover operator. This method makes it possible to enhance the EA’s performance by incorporating situation-specific knowledge.

## 12.10 Applications and Extensions of Fuzzy EAs

It is possible to design fuzzy EAs to address specific issues, including multimodal optimisation challenges. Fuzzy logic may also aid contemporary hybrid metaheuristics in improving their performance, leading to fuzzy hybrid metaheuristics.

### 12.10.1 Multimodal Optimisation Problems

A basic EA will typically converge to a single solution when faced with a problem that has several solutions. To enable EAs to consistently find several optima, a number of techniques have been put forth to sustain a diversified population during the search. Many of these techniques promote the creation of artificial niches through crowding and sharing, however, these techniques also add one or more factors that impact algorithm performance, like the density of crowding or the transfer radius in efficiency transfer. In many cases, solutions with different locations and extents cannot be captured by the uniform specification of niche size without also pushing the population size over acceptable limits.

Thus, it is still necessary to create niching techniques that consistently and profitably identify the greatest niches, irrespective of their size and spacing. FLCs could be helpful in adjusting the parameters related to crowding and sharing techniques. Examples of possible inputs include diversity metrics, the number of extant habitats in the population at large, etc \[[40](#ch12.xhtml#_24032-0872_chp012_ref40)\].

### 12.10.2 Application of Fuzzy Tools to Improve Hybrid Metaheuristics

Many search algorithms that don’t strictly adhere to the principles of a single classical metaheuristic have been reported in recent years. Rather, they seek to maximise the output from a set of metaheuristics (and even other optimisation techniques) that complement and strengthen each other to create a profitable synergy. These methods are frequently called hybrid metaheuristics. The memetic engine (MA) is a renowned example of this kind of engine. To find an arrangement that benefits both, they create a hybrid approach where LS is used within the context of a global search algorithm like EA. The traditional MA technique uses an in-depth searching operation to the EA-obtained responses to increase the population members’ accuracy. A significant  227. component of MAs is the number of physical fitness assessments required by the LS algorithm while it is running (LS intensity). Too short LS intensity could not be competent to investigate the region surrounding the answer and, consequently, hindering the effectiveness of the search, so it is crucial to choose the ideal LS intensity. However, an excessively long LS intensity could fail by needlessly consuming additional fitness tests. A large portion of the information discovered about applying fuzzy logic to enhance EAs could be applied once again to improve the functionality of the most advanced search engines available today.

## 12.11 Conclusion

We provided a comprehensive overview of fuzzy EAs and GFSs in this chapter. Specifically, we reviewed key design tenets for these algorithms, referenced prior research when appropriate, offered a taxonomy for each, and discussed about potential future possibilities and some difficulties for these two research avenues. This study primarily shows that fuzzy EAs and GFSs have combined knowledge bases, making them two exceptional instances of constructive cooperation among soft computing technologies. It also indicates that there are still a lot of fascinating research questions related to these two subjects.
